Qalo SL-1 vs. Garmin Cirqa: How these minimalist wearables compare

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Both screenless bands cost about $200 and work without a subscription, but their specs are pretty different.

Garmin and Qalo both launched screenless fitness bands just 10 weeks apart this year — and also priced them four cents apart at about $200 each. Garmin announced the Cirqa on July 21, and Qalo followed on September 29 with the SL-1. Neither of the bands has a display or requires a paid membership to work, separating them from Whoop, whose bands come with a membership starting at $199 per year.

Looking at their respective spec sheets, Qalo claims the longer battery life while Garmin lists the deeper water rating, more activity types and more training metrics. The Garmin wearable also has an optional paid software tier that Qalo doesn't. We've tested both in depth: our Garmin Cirqa review scored it 7.4 out of 10, while our Qalo SL-1 review gave it 6.8.

Qalo sells the SL-1 for $199.95 with a black silicone strap and a charging dock. Extra straps will set you back $34.95 apiece. No subscription is required to use the band, and the companion app is free.

Garmin's press release says the $199.99 Cirqa tracks its features without the need for a subscription. That said, there is an optional tier called Connect+, which costs $6.99 per month or $69.99 per year. Cirqa's manual lists things like AI-generated insights, nutrition logging, Live Activity Stats, training guidance and a performance dashboard among the features that need the subscription. It also says that guided workouts require a subscription, and that some activities may too. Both bands are eligible for HSA and FSA spending.

Qalo lists the SL-1 at 18 grams and 10.5mm thick but doesn't say whether that weight includes the strap. Garmin's Cirqa is 14.8 grams without the strap and 20.7 total grams with the smaller wristband.

The SL-1 is worn on the wrist, about an inch above the wrist bone, on a silicone strap with a woven one sold separately. Meanwhile, the Cirqa sports a fabric hook-and-loop band that comes in two wrist sizes and can also be worn on the upper arm with a long arm band. Garmin offers four colors, and the Cirqa has one side button, a multicolor status LED and vibration alerts for alarms and a Find My Band feature. Qalo's support pages describe only a charging status light.

Garmin has rated the Cirqa at 5 ATM, which its manual describes as being capable of withstanding pressure equivalent to a depth of 50 meters and includes a Swim activity. In contrast, Qalo's product page says the SL-1 is water-resistant to 10 meters and warns against scuba diving and deep-water pressure. Its support site gives it a IP68 rating. In terms of temperature, the Cirqa's stated operating range is -4 to 140 degrees Fahrenheit, while Qalo lists 14 to 113 degrees Fahrenheit.

Qalo advertises 12 or more days of battery life for the SL-1 and Garmin up to 10 for the Cirqa. In our SL-1 review, the battery still had over half its juice left after a whole week. The Cirqa, meanwhile, charges in up to 90 minutes over a proprietary Garmin cable, while the SL-1 uses a magnetic dock with a USB-C cable. Interestingly, Qalo's docs disagree on how long it takes the SL-1 to charge: the product page says 60 to 120 minutes while the support site says two to three hours.

Both bands come with an optical heart rate sensor and can measure blood oxygen, skin temperature, heart rate variability, stress and sleep stages, and both offer menstrual cycle tracking. Neither has built-in GPS, but the Cirqa can use a paired phone's GPS. Qalo rolls sleep, HRV, movement and stress into a daily "Q Score" from 1 to 100 while Garmin spreads its analyses across Body Battery, Training Readiness, training status, VO2 max and HRV status. The Cirqa lists more than 80 activity types against the SL-1's 54, and the Cirqa can broadcast heart rate to other devices. The SL-1 shares one profile with Qalo's smart rings, which the company also sells without a subscription.

Qalo's product page says the SL-1's 54 activity modes include automatic activity detection, but our review found that the band didn't pick up workouts by itself, not even walks. Meanwhile, the Cirqa's automatic detection worked well enough in our testing that manual recording was rarely needed. Qalo also says its movement tracking is up to 12.5 percent more accurate than "popular alternatives." While our SL-1 review didn't test accuracy, our Cirqa review did count Garmin's reliable accuracy among the band's strengths.

Beyond the specs, we found the Cirqa to be perfectly functional in our testing. That said, the single button was the main irritation, since dismissing the alarm with a mistimed press starts a workout instead. We also found the Garmin Connect app to be pretty heavy on data and light on interpretation, and it was hard to see why the Cirqa costs more than Garmin's own cheaper trackers. The SL-1 fared a little worse. The battery was excellent, still showing 59 percent after a full week, and there's no fee to worry about, but the magnetic clasp on the silicone strap can come loose if a sleeve catches it at the wrong angle, and the app does little beyond aggregating the data it collects.

With both bands at the same price, we'd lean towards the Cirqa, which gets the basics right. The SL-1 would make more sense as a first screenless tracker if it were cheaper.

Nvidia Launches Investigation Into CEO Jensen Huang Following Honeypot Scandal

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

What first emerged as tabloid gossip seems to be escalating into a dangerous scandal for graphics card titans Nvidia. In recent weeks, stories emerged that CEO Jensen Huang fell victim to a honeypot operation. One that could have contributed to $2 million worth of contraband breaking sanctions on China. Now the company says they will be launching a formal investigation into Huang as they become the center point of artificial intelligence.

Nvidiaâs board of directors says it is launching an independent investigation into its CEO, Jensen Huang, after reports in The New York Times and the FT revealed that Huang was likely the target of an intelligence operation run by China.

— Yashar Ali ð (@yasharali.bsky.social) 2026-11-08T20:30:12.500Z

“The NVIDIA Board is initiating an independent investigation in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort,” reads the company statement. “A committee of independent directors, assisted by outside independent counsel, will oversee the investigation.”

In late October, stories began to emerge that Huang was the target of a spy plot, allegedly having an affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang. Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company, has been under investigation since 2024 over the smuggling of Nvidia hardware into China, an increasingly common practice but breach of American sanctions nonetheless. According to the Financial Times, US intelligence alleges that the Megaspeed executive was acting directly on behalf of the Chinese government as a spy, raising concerns that sensitive intel may have been supplied on top of parts. Now having confirmed to the company that the infidelity took place, Nvidia announced that they will launch a formal investigation into their acting CEO.

“I was unfaithful to my wife,” Huang wrote in a company memo. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

While PC gamers are long familiar with Nvidia, the GPU makers have grown into one of the most influential companies in the world, a prime driver behind the ever growing AI sector. While AI firms still struggle to deliver a profit for themselves, Nvidia is one of the biggest benefactors of the industry, manufacturing the parts that make it possible and watching the price tag on components surge. This scarcity and federal-level AI race has also launched efforts to limit Chinaâs access to components, with Washington kicking off dramatic new policies to keep computer components and e-waste from leaving the country.

The news comes as the AI sector seemingly rankles from the results of the American midterm elections. Increased scrutiny and policy from incoming lawmakers seeming to spook the previously excitable investors. Despite the investigation, Nvidia says that Huang will remain as acting CEO. The company also maintains that despite the high scandal, they have “no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised.”

CEO Mike Bilder doesn't see AI as having much to offer Jackbox.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On a recent visit to Jackbox Games HQ here in Chicago, I got to speak with developers and studio leadership about their process, history, and place in the industry. Given, you know, everything, I'm pretty much contractually obligated to ask about AI in conversations like this. Here's what CEO Mike Bilder had to say on the topic:

"The people are what make this product. The comedy, the writing, and the art, all that is done by people, and we have no intention of changing that. I don't think we're experts on what's going on with AI in the industry and any of that. There's obviously uses for tooling and for research, and [for] all that kind of stuff [AI is] incredibly powerful.

"But from a generative asset standpoint, we don't have any intention of replacing people, or people-made editorial and art with it."

From everything we had discussed up to this point, as well as Jackbox's whole deal, I didn't think generative AI would really be in the cards for the studio. But I still appreciated Bilder's straightforward, clear answer on the subject, particularly when the standard playbook these days is fence sitting reminiscent of The Simpsons' "Abortions for some, miniature American flags for others."

Jackbox's games are relatively lightweight from a tech perspective, while the art and writing is their main draw⁠—if anything was noticeably AI-generated, it would put the value of the whole package into question.

The company recently released the Jackbox Party Pack 12 on October 14, and the studio's production values have really progressed since the last entry I had played⁠—Trivia Murder Party 3 makes extensive use of realtime 3D graphics, for example. Jackbox Party Pack 12 is available on pretty much everything, but this is Personal Computer Gamer, so I'll say you can grab it on Steam.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

The two have been together for several years now.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Halle Berry is known for being something of an open book, as she’s shared aspects of her life and career with her fans over the years. (Of course, she’s also known to share the occasional bikini photo or two.) In more recent years, Berry really hasn’t had a problem publicly highlighting her relationship with boyfriend-turned-fiancé Van Hunt. Comments shared by the Oscar-winning actor and Hunt suggest they have a firm relationship. And, apparently, that’s carried right through her ongoing custody battle.

For weeks now, Berry and ex-husband Olivier Martinez have been engaged in a legal skirmish, with the latter having leveled accusations against the X-Men star. Berry (via her lawyer) has since vehemently denied the claims and has appeared in court a few times. By her side has been Van Hunt, who’s been photographed walking with her after the court hearings she’s attended. According to Page Six, Hunt has been a major source of support for Berry amid the legal proceedings. The outlet’s insider says this:

Van has really been Halle’s rock through all of this. He’s been by her side throughout the whole custody battle and restraining order situation.

Hunt and Berry began dating back in 2020 and, before long the singer/songwriter/music producer began making frequent appearances in Berry’s Instagram posts. Although these latest comments are from an insider, they do appear to align with what’s been said and shown of Berry and Hunt’s romance. The source went on to drop further claims about what Hunt’s presence has meant for his partner throughout this time:

It’s obviously been an incredibly difficult and stressful time for her, but he’s been such a calming presence and a huge source of strength. He’s been incredibly supportive through everything, and having someone like him to lean on has made a big difference for her.

Halle Berry Shared How She And Van Hunt Happened: 'I'm Convinced I Manifested My Now Partner'

As previously alluded to Berry and Hunt have appeared together in more than a few of the former’s social media posts over the last several years. For example, years back, Berry posted about their outing at the NY Spring/Summer 2024 fashion show, and she’s also shared sweet tribute posts to Hunt. Berry is even far past the point at which she’s comfortable sharing her and Hunt’s Christmas festivities.

It was in 2025 that Hunt proposed to Berry, though both of them confirmed around that same time that the Bruised actress had yet to formally accept it. Berry explained that after having been married three times, she wasn’t eager to jump into another marriage right away. In her estimation, she and Hunt also didn’t need to “validate” their love in any way. However, earlier this year, Berry clarified that she did accept the proposal but that she and her partner had yet to set a wedding date.

Halle Berry and Van Hunt have emerged as something of a fan-favorite couple on social media, so many take comfort in the notion of the latter comforting his lady amid these legal proceedings. And, as for when or where the pair might actually tie the knot, that remains to be seen.

The Only Great Steven Seagal Movie Is Coming To Netflix, And I Can't Wait To Rewatch

One Flew Over The Cuckoo's Nest Is My Favorite Book, But I Have A Real Issue With The Movie

I Enjoyed Backrooms, But Can We Finally Admit It Wasn't That Scary?

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

My favorite Stephen King adaptation is, without a doubt, Stand by Me. Almost two years ago, we here at CinemaBlend lamented that the Rob Reiner-directed movie was leaving Netflix, but now it’s back, and I definitely plan on watching it again with my Netflix subscription. It’s actually been a lot longer than two years since I last saw Stand by Me, and I’m definitely ready for a rewatch of the movie with a now legendary cast including Wil Wheaton, River Phoenix, Jerry O'Connell, Corey Feldman, Keifer Sutherland, and John Cusack. I have a strong emotional attachment to the movie that outshines any other of King’s book-to-screen adaptations.

I was ten years old when Stand by Me was released in 1986, and I was 11 when I saw it. While the kids in the movie were a touch old, as with any 11-year-old, I thought of myself as slightly older than I actually was. That made me right in the sweet spot for the movie. Sure, I was a child of the '70s/’80s, and the characters in the movies were my parents' generation, but I felt a kinship with them. Plus, because they were my parents' generation, I had a direct connection to the 1950s, having heard all the stories about my mom and dad’s childhoods.

That also meant that I connected with the music in the movie. Director Reiner and music supervisor Celest Ray picked some bangers from the early days of rock n’ roll for the soundtrack, and I loved it. I’m sure I heard the soundtrack before I saw the movie, much like another Boomer nostalgia movie, Good Morning, Vietnam. For kids in the 1980s, the soundtracks to movies like Stand by Me were often our first exposure to them.

The ‘80s were a different time, and seeing Rated-R movies way before we ever should have meant that I had seen a couple of King adaptations before Stand by Me. Movies like Firestarter and Cujo were staples of cable TV, and I’d seen both. I’d also been terrified of both. I didn’t even know Stand by Me was based on a King novella until I saw his name during the opening credits. It blew my mind. I thought he wrote horror stories. Stand by Me definitely wasn’t that, although the dog “Chopper” scared me as much as Cujo did.

Stand by Me is the first adaptation from a King story that showed that other side of the author. The one we all know now from movies and books like this one and The Shawshank Redemption. I had no idea he wrote such heartwarming stories to go along with the ones that kept me up at night, like The Shining and Carrie. Before Stand by Me, all the movies based on his books were like those and Maximum Overdrive, which was another movie I saw around this same time and really scared me, like, a lot.

Stand by Me has no horror at all, and nothing supernatural. Just a deeply personal story about friendship that kids of any era can connect with, just like I did. It's one of the best King movies, despite not being what he's most famous for. I can’t wait to watch it again this weekend.

Hugh Scott is the Syndication Editor for CinemaBlend. Before CinemaBlend, he was the managing editor for Suggest.com and Gossipcop.com, covering celebrity news and debunking false gossip. He has been in the publishing industry for almost two decades, covering pop culture – movies and TV shows, especially – with a keen interest and love for Gen X culture, the older influences on it, and what it has since inspired. He graduated from Boston University with a degree in Political Science but cured himself of the desire to be a politician almost immediately after graduation.

Hollywood heavy hitters turned out to witness Saturday Night Live history on Saturday as comedian Druski became the fist digital creator to host the venerable NBC late-night sketch program.

Durski’s Eddington co-star Emma Stone and newly elected Texas Senator James Talarico were expected to join the host for the curtain call at the end of the show having appeared in the monologue and Cold Open, respectively. But they were not the only celebrity visitors onstage alongside Druski, the SNL cast and musical guest The Strokes. The group included two Oscar winners, Stone and Michael B. Jordan, and one Oscar nominee, Austin Butler. This Semiconductor ETF Just Had Its Worst Day Since March 2020

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The drop hastened the iShares Semiconductor ETF's slide into a bear market.

Technology stocks were drubbed on Thursday, Nov. 5, as investors priced in the impact of Tuesday's midterm election results.

Amid a blue tsunami that saw Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress and a host of gubernatorial victories, fears are rising that data center construction could stall and artificial intelligence (AI) regulations could enter the spotlight. Semiconductor stocks are epicenters of the calamity.

The iShares Semiconductor ETF just posted its worst intraday slide in over six years. Image source: Getty Images.

Just look at the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX +3.51%). The second-largest exchange-traded fund (ETF) in the semiconductor category slid 12.1% on Thursday, notching its worst intraday decline since March 2020, when stocks slumped into a bear market due to the coronavirus pandemic. Let's see if this ETF's weakness is a warning sign or a buy-on-the-dip opportunity.

Unfortunately, the iShares ETF's Nov. 5 slide isn't a one-off event. It comes on the heels of an 8.2% drop on Oct. 30. That is to say, in the span of about a week, this ETF has had its fifth- and seventh-worst intraday showings ever, and that's saying something, because the fund is more than 25 years old. The fund is now down 30% over the past month and trades nearly 38% below its 52-week high. In short, it's in a bear market.

Yes, election results make for an easy scapegoat for chip stocks' recent misfortunes, but there are also some stock-specific issues hindering this ETF. For example, Qualcomm (QCOM +5.53%), the fund's eighth-largest holding, tumbled on Thursday after reporting a 26% decline in fiscal fourth-quarter smartphone chip sales.

Micron Technology (MU +5.12%) is another offender hailing from this ETF's roster. That stock, the iShares fund's fourth-largest component, plunged 17% on Thursday amid fears that memory chip shortages could pinch the broader tech sector and that, if data centers stall, demand for memory chips will slump. Memory chip demand is pertinent to the iShares ETF because the fund holds several other memory names in addition to Micron.

Add in election results that some experts say represent a "total repudiation" of AI, and these are perilous times for semiconductor stocks and related ETFs.

There's no denying that "bloodbath" is an accurate description of the week for semiconductor stocks, but in turbulent times, perspective is most valuable. On that note, investors interested in kicking in the tires on the iShares ETF or individual chip names may be heartened to know that it's not uncommon for markets to immediately overreact to election results before cooler heads prevail over the subsequent weeks and months.

Second, and admittedly this is a wild card, anti-AI/data center stances make for good campaign trail fodder, but post-election reality is different for politicians. That reality includes the burning desire (in most cases) to win the next election, and railing against cash-rich tech companies that can fund opposition in primary or general elections may not be the ticket to future political victory for this year's winners.

So there may be an opportunity with chip stocks and ETFs, and the silver lining is that prospective buyers acting in the near term will benefit from better pricing than was available just a few weeks ago.

Todd Shriber has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Micron Technology, Qualcomm, and iShares Trust-iShares Semiconductor ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Apps to distract you from the endless cycle of doomscrolling

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

You open your phone to check the time or a quick message. The next thing you know, an hour has passed and you’ve scrolled through endless celebrity drama, cat videos, awful news stories, influencer rants, and whatever else the algorithm decided to throw at you.

Even though you probably don’t want to keep wasting your time and energy on this mind-numbing content, you do it again the next day.

Doomscrolling, the habit of spending excessive amounts of time consuming content on social media, has become incredibly widespread. A survey from last year found that 64% of Americans say they doomscroll.

Researchers have warned that doomscrolling can negatively affect several aspects of your well-being, including your mental health and attention span. Spending long periods scrolling can lead to brain fatigue, difficulty focusing, and disrupted sleep. And if a lot of the content you’re consuming is negative or stressful, it can leave you feeling disheartened, anxious, and emotionally drained.

It’s hard to break the cycle of doomscrolling, but there are plenty of apps that provide content that’s engaging and productive.

Of course, you could always read a book or go for a walk (we have a guide on how to stop doomscrolling), but this list is for when you have a few spare minutes and want something to do on your phone that isn’t endless scrolling.

Finch is a self-care pet app that’s designed to help you build healthy habits. You care for a cute virtual pet by completing small daily goals, such as drinking enough water, going on a walk, and practicing mindfulness. You can write about your day, complete a breathing exercise, or send good vibes to a friend by adding them to the app.

The more self-care you do, the more your pet thrives. The app offers a fun way to trade mindless scrolling for small moments of self-care.

Finch is available for free on iOS and Android.

Libby is a free reading app that connects you to your local public library’s digital collection of ebooks, audiobooks, and magazines. All you need is a library card to borrow thousands of titles, which makes it easy to turn screen time into something productive.

You can explore a new book during a commute or while waiting at a doctor’s office, turning moments when your fingers might otherwise itch to scroll into opportunities to read.

Libby is available for free on iOS and Android.

If you want to take a break from doomscrolling and try something creative, Dudel Draw might be a good app for you. Each day, the app gives you a random shape that you turn into a drawing. These daily shapes vary from basic geometrical forms to more complex and abstract designs.

You can also get your friends in on the creativity with some fun competition by comparing your different creations, which could provide a nice break from sending each other endless TikToks or Instagram Reels.

If you still want to feel connected to the world without scrolling through social media, you can check out Radio Garden. The app lets you listen to over 25,000 live radio stations from across the globe.

Once you open the app, you’ll see green dots marking cities and towns. Tap any dot to listen to radio stations broadcasting from that location. You can add favorites or search for radio stations, countries, and places.

Radio Garden is free, but it also offers an ad-free premium plan for $2.99 per month. The app is available on both iOS and Android.

Elevate is an app designed to help improve focus, memory, reading, math skills, and other cognitive abilities. It includes over 40 games to train different abilities you use in everyday life, from reading faster to comparing prices more efficiently.

You can track training streaks and compare your mind’s performance over time.

The app offers a free version with access to three games per day, or a yearly subscription of $39.99 for unlimited access. It’s available on both iOS and Android.

Vocabulary is an app that helps you learn new words every day. You can pick your difficulty level and choose categories that interest you, like emotions, the human body, business, and more. Each word comes with a definition, example sentences, and a guide on how to pronounce it.

The app also includes mini-games to help you review the words you’ve learned. You can set a goal of how many words you want to learn each week and create a regular learning routine.

Vocabulary offers a free trial, and then costs $4.99 per month or $59.99 per year. It’s available on iOS and Android.

If you’re a geography nerd looking to test your knowledge or just want to improve your geography skills, Seterra is perfect for you. The app features over 300 different games to test your map skills. You can test your knowledge of world flags; discover oceans, seas, and rivers; and explore mountain ranges and volcanoes across the globe.

Seterra lets you track your progress across categories and see leaderboards for top scorers for each game.

The app is free and available on both iOS and Android.

The NYT Games app offers several word, logic, and number games that change every day to exercise your mind. You can play the crossword, try the word-guessing game Wordle, group words with a common theme in Connections, see how many words you can make from seven letters in Spelling Bee, and more.

The app costs $5.99 per month for unlimited access and archives, but some games like Wordle, Strands, and the Mini Crossword are available to play for free.

NYT Games is available on both iOS and Android.

If you want to learn a new language but want to try something other than Duolingo, Drops is a good option. The app uses visually engaging mini‑games to help you build vocabulary and common phrases in more than 45 languages, with bite‑sized lessons designed to be completed in about five minutes a day.

Drops is designed for both beginners and experts who want to grow their foreign language vocabulary.

The free version of the app offers five-minute lessons per day. You can get unlimited access and premium features for $11.99 per month or $79.99 per year. The app is available on both iOS and Android.

The one Starlink perk that gives it an edge over traditional internet

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Why satellite internet has a unique advantage over terrestrial providers.

It's debatable as to whether Starlink markets its services properly. Anyone who's seen some of the company's ads probably understands the basics, namely that, regardless of which Starlink setup you think best suits you, you're getting the internet via a satellite dish instead of a cable or phone line. Also, if you choose the right plan, like one of the wildly different $55 plans, you can get internet access virtually anywhere in the world. However, until you dig a little deeper, many details remain mysterious, such as important specs like expected speed.

One of those gaps is why you might want satellite internet when you're not roaming or otherwise isolated. In a country like the US, UK or Canada, it's rare to find a community that doesn't have at least some basic landline option. Given that Starlink can get quite expensive with its equipment costs, there has to be serious incentive to sign up.

For some consumers, there is, and it's an adaptability that traditional internet lacks. It's baked into the nature of Starlink, and it could save your bacon if you value resiliency over raw speed. There are some important exceptions, which we'll get into.

Starlink's network is based around clusters of small satellites inserted into low Earth orbit (LEO). As of October 2026, there were over 11,000 working satellites, flying at altitudes ranging from about 205 to 311 miles (330 to nearly 500 kilometers). SpaceX continues to launch more, whether to expand the network or replace malfunctioning units. Essentially, there should always be a cluster overhead at any given time.

The most obvious advantage of this is that whereas traditional internet connections can be knocked offline for hours by a single cable or server going down, it's rare for Starlink to suffer a similar fate. If one satellite fails, another in the same cluster should be able to pick up the slack. Even sightline obstacles aren't necessarily an issue, since the cluster approach means there's almost always a satellite with a viable angle as long as your dish is positioned properly.

As mentioned, this coverage is only slated to get better. SpaceX recently filed an application with the US Federal Communications Commission to operate up to 100,000 of its Gen3 satellites, following up on a recent approval to expand Gen2 numbers as high as 15,000. If you can afford to install and maintain a dish, your internet access could hypothetically become bulletproof, short of power outages or other rare disruptions.

On paper, Starlink dishes should be extremely resilient. Current-generation models can melt snow and endure temperatures from -40 to 110 degrees Fahrenheit (-40 to 43 degrees Celsius). Properly mounted, they can supposedly withstand heavy rain and winds in excess of 99 miles per hour (about 160 kilometers per hour).

In practice, things don't always work perfectly. Snow takes time to melt, and there are regular, brief disruptions while your dish switches from one satellite to another. The possibility of prolonged outages remains, whether because of extreme weather, some systemic problem on Starlink's end or issues with the rest of the internet. It hardly matters how many satellites are in the sky if a particular trunk of the internet is having trouble with its local ground-based infrastructure.

Some prospective users may want to actively avoid Starlink altogether. Apart from the many controversies involving founder Elon Musk, those momentary signal blips can be terrible for real-time activities like online gaming. In a shooter like Fortnite or Counter-Strike, a split second can mean the difference between landing a headshot or taking one.

Circle Says It Froze $1.1B in USDC Linked to Binance Hack Investigation

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Circle says it froze roughly $1.1 billion in USDC connected with the reported Binance exploit while working with law enforcement. The stablecoin issuer stresses that a wallet freeze does not decide asset ownership or whether funds should ultimately be forfeited.

The response to the enormous reported Binance breach has exposed a difficult but essential distinction in digital finance: tracking money is not the same thing as freezing it, and freezing it is not the same thing as recovering it for a victim.

In a November 5 statement, Circle said it had frozen approximately $1.1 billion in USDC associated with the incident, in coordination with law enforcement. It described an attack involving more than $6 billion in digital assets, while emphasising that the investigation and identification of relevant funds remain ongoing.

USDC is issued by a company that operates an administratively controlled token system. Under certain legal processes, the issuer can freeze specified addresses so tokens cannot move in or out. Bitcoin, by contrast, does not have an issuer with an equivalent global address-freezing switch. That difference can matter enormously in the first minutes after an attack.

But it also raises governance questions. Who is authorised to request a freeze? What evidence is required? How quickly can an incorrect restriction be lifted? Circle says it acts on valid legal process and does not decide for itself whether a crime occurred, whether a wallet’s owner committed an offence or who is entitled to the assets. Those questions belong to law enforcement and the courts.

This makes it important not to count frozen funds as already returned to customers. A frozen balance may remain subject to lengthy investigations, ownership disputes and judicial decisions. The amount is an intervention figure, not a completed reimbursement total.

Modern crypto breaches move across bridges, exchanges, token pairs and custody services. Attackers may split assets among many addresses and convert one token into another to make intervention harder. Defenders need analytics capable of tracing flows across those boundaries, along with responsive institutions that can take action where legal powers exist.

An earlier NewsBTC report on Aave freezing new tBTC activity illustrated how different controls can be used to reduce exposure within a protocol. Circle’s move is not the same kind of action, but both show why the ability to intervene is a design choice with trade-offs.

For investigators, the key issue is coordination. An address identified in one jurisdiction may be connected to a stablecoin issuer or exchange in another. Delays can give attackers more time to swap tokens and fragment holdings. Moving quickly is therefore useful, but speed cannot replace careful identification and due process.

The full scope of the incident, ultimate recoveries and allocation of any frozen funds will require additional verified disclosures. Blockchain traces alone can also create false impressions when commentators assign an address to a person without sufficiently strong evidence. Circle’s account is an issuer’s report of its own intervention, not an independently completed forensic audit of every transfer.

The development offers a lesson beyond this incident. Dollar stablecoins can provide rapid payment settlement and targeted interdiction, yet their compliance controls concentrate important operational powers in issuers. Users, institutions and regulators need to understand both sides of that bargain.

Ohio, Georgia, and Arizona Rank Right Behind Texas for New Data Centers, and Democrats Won All 3

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Texas isn't the only major data center hub that got a little more blue after the midterms.

All eyes were on Texas during the midterms, and artificial intelligence (AI) stocks, especially the neoclouds, took a beating when a Democrat governor won the state. The main fear is that increased AI regulations will make it harder to build data centers in the future, and existing projects may get delayed.

It's a legitimate concern, and Texas isn't the only state that was affected. Ohio, Georgia, and Arizona also saw Democratic victories, and they have been instrumental states for the AI boom. Bears will argue that Democratic victories can turn into a major bottleneck for AI as construction projects are delayed or canceled. Here's what investors should consider.

While governor victories capture headlines, each governor must still answer to their state's House and Senate. Those two political arms introduce more variety that can result in gridlock instead of sweeping AI regulations. Here's how it varies for the four states that have played a vital role in artificial intelligence:

Ohio has the best political profile for tech companies. A gridlock essentially lets existing data center projects continue. Texas and Georgia can still create a gridlock due to Republicans controlling the Senate, but their political leverage isn't as good as Ohio's.

Data centers in Arizona won't face the same political gridlock since Democrats have secured the House and the Senate. Arizona Democrats are in the best position to act swiftly.

Although activism and protests toward data centers have gotten louder in recent months, delaying and canceling these projects comes with downsides that extend beyond lost innovation.

While an anti-big tech stance may cause people to look the other way when hyperscalers face roadblocks, those same data center delays and cancellations have a real impact on jobs.

Politicians on both sides of the aisle love to tout job growth. For instance, former President Biden was enthusiastic about a $3.3 billion data center in Wisconsin since it could create 2,000 permanent jobs once built.

If many data centers are delayed or canceled, it can lead to thousands of people losing their jobs. While regulation may become stricter, kneecapping the industry also translates into lost jobs.

Democrats may not care as much about GDP during this cycle since Trump is still the president, but slowing down AI infrastructure can result in negative GDP growth, since it's the main catalyst powering the economy. A negative GDP growth reading won't be a good look. The Democrats can introduce measures that make their voters happy without uprooting the entire AI trade.

Good PR will be incredibly valuable for neoclouds as the Democrats gain more control of the government in January. Investors can expect to see companies take more steps to financially support local communities beyond new jobs. These initiatives can help them win over residents and provide tangible benefits to the entire community.

Part of that may mean reduced tax breaks, a point of contention that was gaining steam before the midterm results. Some tech companies are also acquiring old infrastructure and converting it into AI data centers. For instance, Terawulf is turning a retired coal plant into an AI data center.

Those types of changes are less disruptive and let AI data center developers focus on job creation. If more neoclouds and colocation developers take this approach, it will be harder for Democrats to issue sweeping changes that stall out the industry. Investors should pay attention to how data center developers are enhancing local communities, because winning over locals will reduce uncertainty with their gigawatt pipelines.

A sample of over 5,000 drives reveals one problem with new HDDs

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Many fail in the first year, and those that do often don’t even warn you there’s a problem

One problem with Hard Disk Drives (HDDs) is mechanical unpredictability. In other words, they can fail because of a factory defect in the first week or chug away quite happily until failing without warning once they get older.

Recent data from the cloud storage giant Backblaze shows roughly 1.5% of all HDDs fail and nearly half of those fail in year one. Additionally, 30% of those failed drives break down without warning. That data is based on its experience managing a huge fleet of over 350,000 storage drives.

These insights are confirmed by a Secure Data Recovery report based on what it learned working with drives that had already failed. As well as confirming similar failure rates, it claims HDDs last 807 days on average, though this only reflects those drives that had already failed.

Failure can be a result of production defects, such as head misalignment or seized motors; accidents, such as power surges or drops; and damage to the drive's internal operating system. Backblaze has been sharing statistics about its hundreds of thousands of drives for years. Its data shows Seagate was the most mechanically reliable across the April-June 2026 period, though some models were more reliable than others. Drives from Hitachi, Seagate and Western Digital tend to lead in similar analyses of failure rates.

The trade-off for the mechanical risk inherent to HDDs is that this kind of storage remains significantly cheaper by GB than other storage media, particularly as demand from AI data centers pushes SSD (solid state drive) storage prices higher. Granted, one reason SSDs are such a success in the AI industry is because they're much faster than HDDs, making it possible for AI systems to work swiftly with very large data sets. Where cost matters more than performance, when it comes to high-capacity storage, the cost advantage is so significant that you can build RAID (Redundant Array of Independent Disks) systems based on HDDs for less than the cost of a single high-capacity SSD.

All the data confirms a connection between HDD failure rate and age, suggesting drives become more prone to fail once they reach seven years old. This implies that if you rely on HDDs in your workflow and need to keep your data, you should plan a replacement schedule for aging drives or invest in additional storage solutions, such as cloud.

Bad as they seem, these failure rates aren't unexpected and can be seen as reflective of the technology itself. One of the reasons Apple pioneered use of SSDs in consumer devices was their relative durability compared to HDDs.

Will HDD technology evolve to become more resilient? The technology is advancing, but the vast majority of drives rely on CMR (Conventional Magnetic Recording) tech, which provides predictable performance. Backblaze is monitoring emerging HDD technologies such as SMR (Shingled Magnetic Recording) and laser-heated HAMR (Heat-Assisted Magnetic Recording) but doesn't currently use any. This suggests the next evolution of HDD tech isn't with us yet.

In the end, you must craft your setup to prepare for failure. Tech pundits have argued for years that we should be combining cloud, local and off-site storage systems to properly secure data against theft, fire and mechanical failure. If you're currently using HDD storage, don't drop it; use these tips to monitor its condition and invest in supplemental storage to guarantee data integrity.

Steve Buscemi And Sam Rockwell Have VERY Different Takes On Getting Killed On-Screen

When I was watching Martin McDonagh’s Wild Horse Nine, a project on the 2026 movie schedule that features a lot of talk about death, I couldn’t help but think about how often some of its stars have died on screen. Specifically, Steve Buscemi has famously kicked the bucket in a ton of projects, and Sam Rockwell has had his fair share of deaths (and kills) too. So, I asked them about all this, and I was shocked that they had very different takes on the matter.

While Steve Buscemi is on the record saying that the on-screen death that amused him most was his death in The Big Lebowski, I asked him who he was most excited to be killed by. He had a pretty great answer too, as he said:

I mean, Tony Soprano. That was a good one. Coen Brothers have done their best, too.

Listen, to die in such a classic TV show like The Sopranos (which you can watch with an HBO Max subscription), and at the hands of Tony Soprano himself, is pretty epic. Plus, as Buscemi mentioned, the Coen Brothers sure have done their best to creatively kill him too. Honestly, it’d be an honor to die on screen at the hands of any of these guys.

However, despite all this, and the fact that Buscemi has died in movies and TV shows what feels like countless times, he’s actually not a huge fan of watching that kind of thing. He brought that up in our conversation when I asked him to reveal something surprising about himself:

I'm pretty squeamish. I don't like watching violence, including my own death scenes. But you know, it's an occupational hazard for me.

Well, while it’s an occupational hazard for him, his Wild Horse Nine co-star, Sam Rockwell, has a totally different take on what it’s like to die on screen.

**Now, beyond this point there are major, and I mean MAJOR, spoilers for Wild Horse Nine. Please continue reading with caution, and go see the movie in theaters. **

Now, ironically, in the critically acclaimed Wild Horse Nine, Sam Rockwell’s character, Lee, is responsible for multiple deaths, including MJ’s, who Steve Buscemi plays, and Chris’ (John Malkovich). However, while the Oscar winner survived this film, he’s died in plenty of other projects, so I asked him about his favorite way he’s died and his favorite person he’s killed. Here’s what he had to say:

Whoa, favorite person I've killed, and favorite movie I've died? I died in Seven Psychopaths. That was pretty fun. Favorite person I've killed is probably John.

Well, notably, while Buscemi called dying on screen an “occupational hazard,” Rockwell was throwing around “fun” as his primary adjective. However, his death in Martin McDonagh’s Seven Psychopaths is objectively “pretty fun”. I mean, it involves a standoff, Rockwell’s Billy getting shot by Woody Harrelson’s Charlie, and one very memorable moment with a Shih Tzu.

Moving on from that, though, he said his favorite person to kill on screen was John Malkovich, which happens in Wild Horse Nine at the very end of the movie. While it’s by no means a fun scene, it is a very meaningful one, and when I asked Malkovich what it was like to be killed by Rockwell, he said:

Well, there you have it, sometimes dying on screen is fun, sometimes it’s great, and other times it's hard to watch. However, no matter how they react to it, all these guys are remarkable actors who are very good at being both alive and dead in the movies.

So, if you’d like to see them do all that, you can catch Wild Horse Nine in theaters now.

Ezra Dyer: Maybe the Saturn Sky Red Line Is Not Quite a Discount Viper, but It Comes Close

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This car is proof that the old GM’s ideas weren’t all bad, as I find out when I take custody of one.

From the November/December 2026 issue of Car and Driver.

Back in 2007, I gave my father-in-law, Roger, a ride in a Dodge Viper test car. He loved it, and I offhandedly said, “If you like this, a Saturn Sky Red Line is 80 percent of the performance for 30 percent of the price.” That was the extent of my salesmanship, so I was surprised a month or so later to learn that he’d gone out and bought a new Sky Red Line—for less than $30,000.

I mean, people ask me for car-buying advice all the time. But the way it usually works is that someone asks my pick for an electric sedan, I tell them to drive a Lucid Air, and then they buy an F-350 Power Stroke. So Roger’s follow-through was both rewarding and unnerving, because now he owned a Sky, and I owned my opinion, which is dangerous. I once had a boss who asked me to name a car that was “overpowered but understated,” and I blurted three words that I came to regret: “Volkswagen Phaeton W-12.” I think soon thereafter the company handbook was amended to make Phaeton recommendations a fireable HR violation.

See all results for used cars for sale near 20147

Fortunately, Roger liked the Sky, the first new GM car he’d purchased since a 1982 Camaro Berlinetta that was so terrible that he swore he’d never buy another GM vehicle. He usually sticks to such decrees, but maybe Saturn got a pass because the brand wasn’t around when his Chevy was putting him on a first-name basis with the local tow-truck dispatchers. In any case, the only problem the Sky ever had pertained to the notorious GM ignition-switch recall, wherein pre-bankruptcy GM’s pathological commitment to cheapness led to ignition cylinders unfit for a Little Tikes Cozy Coupe and thus occasional random engine shutdowns. This happened to Roger once on a back road, but he was unbothered. “I just put it in neutral, restarted it, and kept driving,” he told me. “Didn’t even stop.” I guess the Camaro might have permanently recalibrated his expectations for build quality.

Eventually, Roger stopped driving, but he still liked the Sky, so it sat in his driveway long enough for the neighbors to plant hedges so they wouldn’t have to look at it. But where they saw a dented, pollen-crusted car with two flat tires, I saw a dented, pollen-crusted car with two flat tires and a 260-hp turbo four, a stick shift, rear-wheel drive, and a limited-slip differential. Roger said that if I could get it going, I could have it for the bargain price of “get it out of my driveway.”

So, one day, I arrived with a battery jump pack, an air compressor, and five gallons of fresh gas. The questionable tires held air, and the electrics powered up. I splashed in some fuel and turned the key. The Sky fired up like it had been parked an hour before, its digital odometer showing just 43,000 miles. I put it in neutral, let out the clutch, and it immediately stalled because it wasn’t actually in neutral. Turns out you can wiggle the Red Line shifter side to side quite a bit even when it’s in second gear.

See all results for used cars for sale near 20147

Other revelations mostly pertained to ran-when-parked status. The flat-spotted tires and various wheel-on-curb encounters gave the Sky the ride and directional stability of a grocery cart you pull out of the corral and immediately swap for a different one. The battery wouldn’t hold a charge, the interior smelled like a cologne called Old Must, and the A/C didn’t blow cold. But the scrappy Red Line ran like a champ, the turbo issuing a jaunty chirp on upshifts and all 260 horses feeling ready to gallop. It just needed a little deferred maintenance.

I bought some Pirelli P Zeros and got an alignment, since I’ve found that cars drive better with round tires all pointed in the same direction. I changed the oil and recharged the A/C. Next, I tackled the battery, which is mounted somewhere near Earth’s molten core. Accessing it requires removing the right front fender, and I got impatient and only mostly unbolted it, figuring I could bend the flexible composite panel enough to get the battery out. That’s how I tore a fender while replacing the battery, which is not a sequence of words I expected to write back when I told Roger that a Sky Red Line is a discount Viper.

But I stand by my assessment. The Sky was, and is, huge fun. It’s agile and powerful, low-slung and eager to carve a corner. It makes any drive feel like Bowser and Koopa Troopa should be trying to beat you to the finish line of Rainbow Road. The critiques that dogged the Sky when it was new—cheap interior, little storage space, poor visibility—seem less important now. Saturn ran out of time to perfect the Sky, but it’s perfect enough. Dents and all.

Ezra Dyer is a Car and Driver senior editor and columnist. He's now based in North Carolina but still remembers how to turn right. He owns a 2009 GEM e4 and once drove 206 mph. Those facts are mutually exclusive.

Nvidia investigates CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with alleged Chinese spy

📅 2026-11-08  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia has opened an investigation into co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a businesswoman who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy.

U.S. intelligence agencies have expressed concerns that the woman, Alice Huang, who is affiliated with a cloud company and an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed, has been passing information to Beijing, according to reports in The New York Times and the Financial Times.

Huang told Nvidia staff in an email on Sunday that he had an affair with the woman and that Nvidia's board of directors had opened an independent investigation into the spying allegations.

"I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," Huang wrote in the message. "I am deeply sorry."

An Nvidia spokesperson said that Huang was committed to continuing as CEO of Nvidia without interruption.

In a statement, the Nvidia board said it would investigate the allegations that Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort.

"While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," Nvidia's board said in a statement.

Read Huang's email to Nvidia employees and the company board of directors' statement here.

The allegations come as Nvidia has become the center of a trade dispute between China and the U.S. as AI is seen as increasingly critical to national security.

The U.S. restricts exports of Nvidia's most advanced AI chips to China, though it has approved exports of certain older, slower models.

Megaspeed, which Ms. Huang is affiliated with, has been accused of helping restricted Chinese companies acquire Nvidia AI chips. Nvidia representatives previously said that Megaspeed had already been investigated for evading export controls.

Nvidia's Huang has pushed for broader AI chip exports to China, saying it is better for the U.S. if the world uses and builds on American technology. He has also aligned with President Donald Trump on American artificial intelligence policy and its infrastructure buildout.

In his note to staff, Huang said he was "proud and grateful" to be an American.

Three House Republican committee chairs sent a letter to Huang on Wednesday asking him to sit for a closed-door briefing with lawmakers on Nov. 18.

Nvidia reports third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17 and will hold a major conference in Washington, D.C., at the end of the month.

Chipmaker's probe comes as the CEO admits to having an affair with the suspected spy.

Nvidia on Sunday said it’s opening an investigation into whether Jensen Huang, the company’s chief executive officer and founder, was targeted by a foreign intelligence effort after Huang admitted having an affair with a business executive who was reportedly a Chinese spy.

The announcement of any investigation follows a series of reports that Huang had developed a “close relationship” with Alice Huang, no relation to Jensen Huang, who is a Chinese citizen and an executive at Megaspeed. Megaspeed is a Singapore-based data center company that buys chips from Nvidia to run data centers that sell computing power to Chinese AI labs.

The New York Times reported in late October that US intelligence agencies had Alice Huang under electronic surveillance for allegedly facilitating the sale of advanced Nvidia chips to China in violation of US law. During that surveillance, officials discovered she was also allegedly funneling information to the Chinese government in Beijing.

The announcement comes as Nvidia has solidified its position as a leader in the AI boom. The cutting-edge computer chips that Nvidia produces are what make AI and AI data centers possible, and that has made Nvidia the world’s most valuable company by market cap — $5 trillion this year.

“Today’s news will not slow Nvidia,” an Nvidia spokesperson said. “Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption.”

A committee of independent directors, working with outside counsel, will oversee the investigation, the board said in a statement. The company added that it “has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board’s inquiry will examine all available information.”

In his own memo to Nvidia employees, Huang said he was “deeply sorry” for the affair. “I was unfaithful to my wife,” he wrote. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did.” Huang, 63, added that he will cooperate fully with the board’s investigation.

The company had previously characterized Jensen Huang’s interactions with Alice Huang as “not unusual or unexpected” given that the CEO “talks with thousands of customer executives every year.”

NAR sees pent-up demand waiting on lower mortgage rates, better sentiment

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NAR economist Lawrence Yun said pent-up housing demand could be released if mortgage rates ease, inventory improves, and confidence rebounds. NAR data show existing-home sales near 4M, first-time buyers at 22%, and months’ supply at its highest since 2015.  AI Summary

The U.S. housing market is building “significant” pent-up demand that could be released if mortgage rates ease, inventory improves and consumer confidence rebounds, National Association of Realtors’ (NAR) chief economist Lawrence Yun told attendees Friday at NAR’s NXT in New Orleans.

Yun contrasted today’s sales environment with the year 2000, noting that the U.S. population has grown from 281 million to 350 million and payroll jobs from 132 million to 159 million over that period. Housing wealth has ballooned from $7 trillion to $35 trillion, yet existing-home sales are tracking near 4 million this year, well below the 5.2 million homes sold in 2000, he said, citing NAR data.

“Affordability is tough, but people’s incomes are rising, and if somehow mortgage rates go down, think about the potential housing demand that could be released to the marketplace,” Yun said.

On the supply side, Yun said inventory has risen as sales have slowed. Months’ supply is now at its highest level since 2015, and the median home took 33 days to go from listing to contract in September, according to NAR.

“Homes are staying on the market a little longer than before,” Yun said. “With longer days on market, you have to be realistic to the market condition. Otherwise, buyers will reject the homes on the market.”

Yun pointed to consumer psychology as a key missing ingredient. Consumer sentiment has fallen to roughly half its 2000 level and is now worse than during the Great Recession, even as the labor market has added jobs.

“If we can boost confidence levels, maybe that can also change the dynamics,” Yun said.

Jessica Lautz, NAR deputy chief economist and vice president of research, presented findings from the trade group’s newly released 2026 Profile of Home Buyers and Sellers. First-time buyers made up 22% of all buyers, near the series low, and the median age of first-time buyers held at a record-high 40.

Those numbers underscore how younger households are delaying homeownership, even as they form households and enter peak earning years. For lenders and real estate brokerages, that means fewer entry-level transactions and more emphasis on move-up or equity-rich buyers.

“First-time home buyers are struggling,” Lautz said. “They’re not struggling just with housing affordability. They’re also struggling with rent. They’re struggling with student loan debt, and a rising issue for first-time home buyers is childcare costs.”

Lautz noted that sellers reported staying in their homes for a median of 12 years before selling, the longest tenure on record for the survey.

“The golden handcuffs of low interest rate mortgages is true. People are holding on to those properties for longer periods of time,” she said. “It’s why we need to build more housing inventory—especially affordable housing inventory—for the potential first-time home buyers who are out there.”

Despite technology and direct-to-consumer platforms, use of licensed professionals in transactions remained strong, according to NAR’s survey. A reported 90% of buyers purchased through an agent, the highest share in four decades, and a record 92% of sellers used an agent.

“Sellers want agents to price that home competitively, find that qualified buyer, help with marketing, and make sure they get to the closing table in a time frame that they need to do so,” Lautz said.

For real estate agents and brokers, NAR’s data point to a market where transaction volume is constrained more by affordability, rates and sentiment than by underlying demand. A larger population, more jobs and higher housing wealth suggest that activity could accelerate quickly if mortgage rates retreat and confidence improves.

For lenders, a 22% first-time buyer share and a median first-time buyer age of 40 highlight both near-term headwinds and an eventual backlog of delayed demand. Households that are renting longer and juggling student debt and childcare may respond strongly to any easing in rates, pricing or down payment constraints.

For builders and developers, Lautz’s comments reinforce the policy and business case for adding affordable inventory. With existing owners locked in by sub-4% mortgages and staying put for a median 12 years, new construction remains one of the few levers to ease the entry-level shortage.

Yun’s and Lautz’s remarks come as the industry also adjusts to post-settlement commission rule changes and elevated borrowing costs, making accurate pricing, counseling on affordability and clear communication with consumers more central to winning business in 2027 and beyond.

Hunters Can’t Really Improve Wild Whitetail Genetics. Here’s Why

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 8, 2026 4:52 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

It was the year we’d been waiting for at the family deer camp. All summer and fall we pulled trail camera photos of more—and bigger—bucks than had ever been seen on the property. It was a far cry from the early 90s, when the bucks my dad and uncles did spot tended to have a signature narrow spread with tall, skinny tines. Now, nearly 30 years later, we’re finally seeing a caliber of deer we weren’t even sure the property was capable of supporting. It’s taken a lot of intensive habitat improvement and discipline, but it’s working.

Still, there was probably a faster way to get here. It should have taken years, not decades, to improve our whitetail habitat. And there are still things we’re trying to figure out—like how to kill those bucks now that we’ve got them. So, I decided to find out what a lot of whitetail hunters get wrong by talking to some hunters who have been succeeding at the management game for a long time.

Typically, a cull buck is one that deer managers have identified as genetically inferior and should be removed from the wild herd. This might be a deer whose antlers are too small or too funky to keep in the gene pool. Studies show that killing these deer solely on the basis of selective harvest (rather than for the traditional reasons of fun, meat, etc.) is ultimately a waste of a good buck tag.

“Many hunters simply believe that you can influence antler quality through genetics in the wild,” says Kip Adams, chief conservation officer of the National Deer Association (formerly QDMA). “We know very clearly that yes, behind a fence—when you can pick which buck is doing the breeding and which doe is doing the breeding—[you] absolutely can influence the genetics. In the wild, it’s very clear we cannot do that.”

This is for a few reasons. For one, does are contributing half the antler DNA to buck fawns. (If you can identify which does have favorable antler genes from your treestand, please share with the rest of the class.) Also, the rut doesn’t lead to quite as many fawns as you might think.

“It’s not like the biggest buck out there dominates all the does and sires the most fawns,” Adams says, noting that the average U.S. fawn recruitment right now is .59, or less than one fawn per doe. “Research shows that most bucks sire very few fawns during the course of their life. We can’t dominate the gene pool that way.”

“We can’t predict what a buck’s son will have—from an antler standpoint—by looking at a buck’s antlers,” says Adams.

Let’s say that again for the hunters in the back: Just because a buck has big antlers doesn’t mean his offspring will. And just because a buck has small antlers doesn’t mean his offspring will have small antlers. (Part of that goes back to the fact that you can’t tell what kind of antler genes a doe is carrying around.)

That all boils down to Adams’ original point: A hunter cannot influence the genetics of a wild deer herd. Selective buck culling to optimize antler size doesn’t work. So, what can hunters do, if they want to shoot bigger bucks? It’s a simple two-part plan rooted in the classic QDM refrain. First, let deer get older.

“We tend to see and hear about more bigger bucks than ever before because older deer are encompassing a higher percentage of the total buck harvest today than ever before,” Adams says. “Hunters today are letting deer get older at unprecedented levels, and that’s the reason that, across the whitetails’ range, we see, hear of, photograph, and shoot bigger deer than ever before.”

The 2021 Deer Report reveals that during the 2019-20 deer season, 39 percent of all antlered bucks harvested were at least 3.5 years old. That’s the highest national average ever recorded.

The second thing you can do if you want to increase antler size? Increase the available nutrition for deer. This means increasing habitat quality, decreasing the number of deer using the habitat (that is, shooting more does), or both.

It’s all good and well to recommend passing bucks to let them mature, but what does that look like on the ground? Some of the most successful big deer hunters follow this advice, and with good results.

Brothers Brent and Justin Cearlock, two of the Illinois hunters behind the world-record whitetail, enjoy considerable success year after year. Part of this is because of where they live, in the prime big-buck county of eastern Illinois. But it’s also because they pass more bucks than they shoot, even with two buck tags apiece per season.

Read Next: Two Years After the World-Record Buck, the Cearlock Brothers Are Still on a Roll

“I have no intention of shooting a deer younger than five, unless it has just a substantially huge rack,” says Justin, who mostly relies on trail camera history to help him establish age estimates. “I don’t care if I think the deer is four. I’m just going to let him get to five, just to see what happens.”

This past season, Brent made the call on a stud buck that didn’t meet this age threshold. Instead of picking up his bow that morning, he grabbed his phone and shot some video.

“I passed a phenomenal deer—the biggest buck I’ve ever let walk in my life,” Brent says. “He was already in the mid-160s, flirting with the 170-inch mark as a 4-year-old deer. I called him Will, because I didn’t know if I would have the willpower to pass him.”

The guys could only imagine what Will might look like in fall 2021. Brent ended up killing a lower-scoring buck that same day: a deer that was at least seven years old, and that he had history with.

“We’re very fortunate to have access to the properties we do. If you don’t have good properties, you’re not going to shoot big deer,” Brent says. “But we do put a lot of effort into it and we pass deer that most people probably wouldn’t pass. There are a lot of guys that say they’ll pass a deer like that. But when they’re standing there and it’s getting dark, they’re like, I’m shooting that deer.”

Brent didn’t regret his decision—the older buck meant a lot to him—but he couldn’t help feeling torn when he learned a neighbor shot Will a few weeks later.

“Passing a big one comes with risk, and I am genuinely happy for the other hunter to get a great buck like that,” Brent says. “But I’d be lying if I said part of me isn’t sincerely disappointed that we just lost what we thought might be our No. 1 target buck next year.”

Often the decision to pass a deer is fraught because of the property itself. Other hunters with permission and neighboring hunters are just two of the many factors that reduce a deer’s odds of surviving into the next season. And the Cearlocks don’t begrudge anyone who wants to shoot younger bucks: They’ve been those guys themselves, earlier in their hunting careers. But the knowledge that a neighbor might shoot a buck isn’t enough to make them abandon their management strategy in the heat of the moment.

“There’s a lot of things that can happen,” Brent acknowledges. “But if you shoot them, they’re definitely not going to make it to five.”

This past deer season, Rick Dahl secured access to a new whitetail property in central Missouri. Dahl believes the new farm, which is in the same county as the parcels he currently hunts, actually holds better potential for bigger bucks. The new property is promising for a few reasons, including its superior soil quality, fewer neighbors to compete with, and the anecdotal evidence that big bucks are being harvested nearby. The fact that the new property doesn’t have any shooter bucks on it yet doesn’t seem to faze him.

“The food supply is very limited on the new farm,” Dahl says, who is a lifelong Missouri deer hunter and chairman of the NDA board. “[But] it’s in the right area and, if we give the deer there what they need to eat, time will tell whether we’ll be able to produce a bigger-antlered deer there.”

Although the property has fertile soil, Dahl says the food supply is limited because it hasn’t been managed for deer yet. A mature forest canopy has prevented understory growth, and many of the tillable fields were used as cattle pasture. Dahl’s no biologist, but he still knows good soil is important for growing big deer.

Dahl conducted a trail camera survey late last year, which revealed a relatively even age-class distribution of deer—and some clues to support his hunch that nutrition is currently a limiting factor for antler growth. The photos of yearling bucks seem to be mostly spikes, instead of fork-horns. And, with the exception of one deer with an extra point, not a single buck on the property seemed to have more than eight points.

“That was really interesting to me,” Dahl says, who noticed four bucks in the 4- and 5-year-old age class that probably scored 140 inches. “It takes a big 8-pointer to score like that. So, there’s clearly an 8-point genetic thing going on there. I will be really interested to see what transpires over the next three to five years as we begin to introduce more food.”

Read Next: 9 Ways to Score Permission on Private Property

What confused me about this particular endeavor—investing time and money in a whole new property that’s going to need careful timber, habitat, and food plot management—is that Dahl currently has a 4.5-year-old minimum age limit agreement with his hunting buddies on the excellent properties they’ve already got. Why not just raise the age limit for shooter bucks to five, like the Cearlock brothers?

“I’m at the point where I think five and a half is the right age [for me],” Dahl says. “But I also realize that everybody’s at a different stage in the maturation process that we all go through as hunters. My hunting partner is a perfect example. He hasn’t shot a lot of bucks. And if we say we’re not going to shoot anything less than five years old, well, what are we doing? We just went from five deer out there we can hunt to two. At some point you’ve restricted yourself. And for me, if there’s only one deer on the farm that I’m willing to shoot, then the question becomes, ‘Is it really fun anymore?’ And maybe that deer jumps the fence and gets shot by the neighbor on October 30. What do you do? Are you just done?”

Happily for Dahl, he doesn’t have to pick. He can tinker with the new farm, and see if those nutritional improvements eventually support the 4.5-year-old bucks he’s interested in.

The Northeast isn’t exactly known for dedicated deer management. This is a combination of multiple factors, including high hunter numbers in states like Pennsylvania and poorer soil quality. But Neil Dougherty, the president of NorthCountry Whitetails consulting services, finds the challenges of creating QDM programs for clients that much more rewarding.

“Iowa is like Disneyland—it’s fake,” Dougherty laughs. “It’s such a wonderful place in those areas where deer have the ability to explode like that, but it’s not the everyman’s hunt. It’s such a foreign thing to most people who are trying to play this deer game.”

When Dougherty examines a deer property, he takes into account 17 different factors—from native habitat to huntability—to determine the property’s potential. He can almost always improve a property to help “unlock the genetic potential” of the deer that are there. But he’s not a magician, either.

“There’s a magic intersection with the amount of food that’s produced on a property, and the amount of deer that are there to consume that food,” Dougherty says, “The problem is, in some big woods country, that could be eight deer per square mile. That kind of hunting sucks.”

While almost all the properties Dougherty works on need some serious rehabilitation of the natural habitat, the biggest problem with a new property usually lies not with the land, but the client.

“When I show up, there’s almost always a hunting strategy that’s flawed,” Dougherty says. “Just about every property has a hunting strategy that does not lend itself to the intersection of the hunter coming in contact with 5-year-old deer. [Those hunters] just haven’t been exposed to 5-year-old deer. They don’t understand what it takes to speak the same language.”

If you broke down those flaws, Dougherty estimates poor understanding of the wind is to blame about 40 percent of the time. That’s a big improvement compared to even 10 years ago. Today’s hunters are generally much more sophisticated at hunting downwind, he says, and being aware of steady or swirling wind patterns.

“Access is the biggest issue I’m finding. It’s super hard to get out of these spots—getting in isn’t that hard to do, but getting out in the dark and not blowing up the area? That requires a lot of discipline. And sometimes we’re limited by the physical characteristics of a property.”

While Dougherty is a habitat expert, his consulting work involves much more than recommending where to put food plots and what to plant in them. At the end of the day, he’s teaching clients how to hunt.

“I would encourage people to not get caught up in this whole genetic thing,” Dougherty advises. “Learn what your [deer] potential is on the property you hunt, know what you’re doing when it comes to habitat, and just be happy with what you’re doing.”

So, even if the stars align and you end up with big deer consistently using your property, the question remains: Can you kill one of them?

As it turns out, I couldn’t last fall. When I did finally get the biggest buck on the place in front of me, I choked. Painful lesson though it was, it was a reminder that managing for mature deer isn’t like shooting fish in a barrel. Sure, it can be a lot easier when you’ve got 5,000 acres and no neighbors or public land for miles around. But that’s unrealistic for most hunters. Even some basic habitat work and personal discipline can improve the number and quality of deer where you hunt.

Natalie Krebs is the Executive Editor of Outdoor Life, where she tackles everything from reporting digital features to producing podcast episodes. Originally from Missouri, she currently lives in northwest Arkansas with her bird dog, Hatchet.
