What NAR’s new MLS policy options mean for data feeds and licensing

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NAR adopted 4 MLS policy recommendations at NAR NXT 2026, including an optional policy that lets MLSs seek up to 50% copyright interest in certain listing content with opt outs. Additional updates address local rules for data feeds, permitted uses tied to brokerage or valuation services, and MLS Committee qualifications.  AI Summary

The National Association of Realtors (NAR) has approved four multiple listing service (MLS) policy recommendations that increase MLSs’ options to claim copyright in listing content, expand local flexibility on data feeds, clarify permissible uses of MLS data and update qualifications for NAR’s MLS Committee.

The recommendations from NAR’s Multiple Listing Issues and Policies Committee were considered and adopted at NAR NXT 2026 conference on Sunday and are tied to the trade group’s 2026-2028 strategic plan to modernize MLS policies, the association said in an announcement Sunday.

The most consequential change for brokers and MLS operators is a revision to MLS Policy Statement 7.85 and the creation of a new optional policy on copyright protection of MLS data.

Under the revised policy, MLSs may, at their discretion, require up to a 50% copyright interest in certain listing content to strengthen copyright enforcement, subject to quarterly copyright registration requirements. MLSs must give participants the right to opt out of assigning a copyright interest to the MLS, and brokers retain the right to independently use and license their own listing content.

Copyright ownership and enforcement have become more important as listing photos and data are syndicated across portals, IDX sites and marketing platforms, and as MLSs face more frequent questions and disputes about unauthorized scraping and reuse of listing content.

NAR also adopted an optional MLS policy on MLS data feed services. The policy allows MLSs to establish local eligibility and licensing requirements for data feeds, as long as those rules are reasonable, objective and applied equally to all participants.

Even with new local rules, participants must retain access to MLS listing information and data feeds for their own listings.

For brokers and vendors, this could affect how quickly and under what conditions they can obtain broader data feeds for tools such as brokerage websites, back-office systems and analytics products, depending on how individual MLSs choose to implement the optional policy.

A new MLS policy statement reinforces that the use of MLS data must comply with applicable MLS participation or data licensing agreements and must be connected to real estate brokerage services, the preparation of appraisals or other valuations of real property.

NAR said this is intended to address concerns around unauthorized use of MLS content, including use cases that are not connected to providing brokerage or valuation services.

For MLSs, this language provides additional backing for enforcement actions against parties that use listing data for unrelated commercial purposes. For brokers and appraisers, it underscores the need to align technology partnerships and data workflows with the scope of their MLS agreements.

NAR also updated the qualification criteria for service on its MLS Committee. The changes are meant to better reflect today’s MLS environment and ensure the committee includes members with relevant industry expertise and perspectives.

While specific qualification details were not outlined in the announcement, changes to committee composition can influence how future policy recommendations are shaped and how regional and business-model interests are represented at the national level.

“These updates reflect our commitment to ensuring MLS policies remain relevant, effective, and responsive to the needs of today’s real estate industry,” Nykia Wright, the CEO of NAR and a 2026 HousingWire Woman of Influence and Vanguard award winner, said in the announcement. “In line with our 2026–2028 Strategic Plan and significant member feedback, we will continue working collaboratively with MLSs, brokers, members, and other industry partners to support a clear understanding and implementation of these changes.”

Because several of the policies are optional, local MLSs will have to decide whether and how to adopt them. MLS executives and boards will likely review their copyright strategies, data feed agreements and committee engagement structures in light of the new framework.

Brokers, teams and technology vendors should review their MLS participation and data licensing agreements to understand how any local policy changes could affect website data feeds, marketing uses of listing content and third-party integrations.

Your Mac has been keeping a file on your family

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Since macOS 13 shipped in 2022, every Mac has run two daemons whose only job is to build a graph about you. Who you are, who you know, how those people relate to you and to each other, how you communicate, where you go, what you do and when. The daemons are called knowledgeconstructiond and intelligenceplatformd, and the closest thing Apple has ever documented about them is a two-sentence man page.

A security duo going by The Bishop and The Knight took the system apart in October and published the anatomy. The graph lives in ~/Library/IntelligencePlatform, fifty-one plaintext SQLite databases protected by macOS's Full Disk Access permission. The core one, graph.db, is a triple store: 181 entity classes, 278 predicates, and a confidence score on every row. What goes into it is basically everything the OS can see. Contacts. Photos, with faces. Calendar titles and attendees. Family Sharing, including the age of every member. The emergency contact in your Medical ID. Events and people Siri Suggestions digs out of your mail and messages. Apps donated through App Intents. Beside the graph sit tables for who appears with whom in your photos, a communication log without content, location and movement, and a stream of device signals: screen locked, Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, charging, ambient light.

Then there is the part the HN headline picked up: a pair of XGBoost classifiers, shipped as CoreML models, that decide who counts as family. They read 144 features and output a probability for seventeen relationship columns per person, recomputed every day. Twenty of the features are identity: same last name, shared home address, an iCloud Family flag, age difference. Forty are communication patterns. Eighty-four come from photographs.

One shipped feature name is worth reading twice: photosPersonPosteriorProbabilityGivenSaturdayNight. A real input to a real classifier, and its job is deciding whether someone is your sister.

The scores look mundane until you look at what produced them. A mother scored 0.960, the ceiling of that column. Nobody typed it. The post counts nine independent signals that land on the same person: the partner column, a composite score, a household entity, the Family Sharing token, an iCloud flag, top twelve-month contact, a Photos face cluster, a Contacts record, a reachable handle. The model joined them and concluded "partner" for someone the owner had never labeled.

The joins are where it stops being about you. From one store, the authors worked out that there is a child in the household and roughly how old, which daycare the child attends along with both phone numbers, where the family will be and between which hours on a given Thursday, and which password manager is installed. A relationship is intelligence one level above the rows it was inferred from.

Deletion does not fully retract any of it. The live graph drops a contact, but a second table, expired_stable_graph, keeps the retracted rows with their confidence and sources, about forty-eight hours by the authors' measurement, longer on their own machines. The scorecard still scored two people a day after the graph had dropped them, people it knew only from a calendar invitation.

The authors also run a thought experiment that applies to any personalized system: what happens when this file ends up in the wrong hands, paired with a local model that has had its guardrails stripped. They call the graph a privacy single point of failure, and they play the scenario out in a second post.

To be fair to Apple: everything happens on device. The databases sit behind the Full Disk Access permission, so other apps cannot read them without the user approving it. Apple's researchers published a paper on the graph techniques back in 2023. And the same store exists on iOS, where a forensics tool called iLEAPP has been reading it since September.

The fair criticism is narrower and harder to wave off. The depth of the inference is undocumented. The kinship classifier is mentioned nowhere. And the graph does not just hold your data; it holds data about everyone who has ever emailed you, appeared in your photos, or been invited to a calendar event, and none of those people were asked.

The authors open-sourced a dashboard called iKnow that reads your own graph locally, if you want to see the file your Mac keeps on you.

Agent instructions: in the repo or in the tool?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There are two places to put the standing rules you want a coding agent to follow: in a file in the repo, or built into the tool that runs the agent. I've ended up with both, and they trade off differently.

In the repo, the rules are versioned. They show up in pull requests, the whole team sees them, and they travel with a clone. The cost is that you write and maintain them per project, and they're only as good as the last person who edited them.

In the tool, they work in every project and for every agent with no setup. The cost is that they're invisible: nobody reviews a diff of them, and they can clash with how a particular repo does things.

Styx does the second kind. It builds in guidance from a few public repos: Ponytail for coding, Impeccable for interfaces, design-critique for reviewing a design, and architecture-designer for system design. Only the core rules are included, each applies only to that kind of work, and your project's own design system and rules come first.

That last part is the rule I'd defend: when the two disagree, the repo wins, because it knows the project and the tool doesn't.

Do you agree, or do you let the tool override the repo?

Pydio Cells access needs a PAT, workspace, and public-share inventory

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Pydio Cells combines identity, file storage, workspaces, OAuth clients, and public sharing. A person who reaches the login page may be blocked before seeing any files, while a leaked personal access token or public link may bypass the browser flow entirely. An exposure assessment that only records the login page leaves the important paths out.

On 2026-11-08, ZoomEye count-only observations returned 0 matches for app="Pydio Cells", 1,470 for title="Pydio Cells", and 2,397 for http.body="Pydio Cells". The query scope was sub_type=all, page 1, and page size 1. The title and body counts are close enough to be useful leads, but neither proves that a host is configured with public shares or weak tokens.

Pydio documents personal access tokens as an alternative to the standard OAuth flow. A token can replace a bearer access token when calling the REST API. It can also be restricted to a specific node with a scope in the form node:NODE_UUID:PERMISSION, where the permission can include read or write.

That scope is useful only when it is applied to the right node and the underlying user still has access to that node. The token should not be treated as an independent grant of filesystem authority. If the user is removed from a workspace or loses a role, the token should lose the effective access as well. Review both the token's scope and the roles that determine the user's workspace ACLs.

Workspaces define which data a user can see through the application. Public links and shares can expose selected data to people who do not have a Cells account. The sharing model may include link permissions and visibility settings. Each share is a capability that should have an owner, a purpose, an expiration where supported, and a revocation path.

Inventory links that are reachable without authentication, including links that are unlisted rather than indexed. A link that does not appear in search results is still reachable by anyone who receives it. Check whether the link grants read only, write, or upload permission and whether the underlying workspace changed after the link was created.

Start from the public host and identify the OAuth, API, and share routes. Confirm that anonymous users can reach only the intended login or share surfaces. Test an API token in a separate test account and verify that it cannot read a workspace the account is not assigned to.

Review user roles, group membership, workspace ACLs, and administrator roles. Then list active personal access tokens by owner and creation date. Revoke tokens that have no owner or no documented consumer. Rotate secrets that were stored in CI logs, browser storage, or public repositories.

For shares, record the creator, target node, permission, and expiration. Test a link after removing the creator's access. Decide whether the link should continue as an independent capability or fail with the user. That policy should be explicit.

Search: ZoomEye title matches for Pydio Cells

Pydio's value comes from flexible delegation. The security work is to inventory every delegation separately: the OAuth client, the personal token, the workspace ACL, and the public share.

Virginia Layoffs November 2026: WARN Numbers Explained

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 8 • Originally published at layoffatlas.com

Virginia holds fifth place in the current national layoff picture. In the 90-day window tracked by Layoff Atlas, Virginia accounts for 2,546 reported workers across its WARN filings, behind California (21,514), Washington (17,411), Illinois (3,548) and Texas (2,750) — about 4% of the 65,089 workers reported nationwide. California and Washington together hold roughly 60% of that total.

This is a November refresh of Virginia's numbers, last spotlighted on October 24. Here is where the state stands now, what changed, and how to read the numbers.

Virginia's full imported archive now holds 2,195 non-cancelled WARN notices reporting 255,368 workers affected. In the last 365 days, the archive contains 74 eligible official filings reporting 8,917 workers, with no worker counts unavailable. The newest official notice date is October 30, 2026. Sixteen notices have future effective dates, meaning layoffs that have been announced but have not yet taken effect.

So far in 2026, Virginia has 67 notices reporting 8,394 workers. That works out to about 125 workers per filing on average. The state's 2026 total is already close to its full-year 2024 total of 63 notices and 8,706 workers, and it sits below 2025, when Virginia recorded 112 notices and 12,067 workers. With almost two months of the year left, 2026 is on course to land between those two years.

The mix of sources behind Virginia's numbers is nearly even: 1,138 direct government records and 1,057 attributed secondary records appear in the full archive. Recent official event types are layoffs and closures.

Compared with the October 24 snapshot, Virginia's 2026 figures have grown. Notices for the year rose from 61 to 67, and reported workers rose from 7,735 to 8,394 — an increase of 659 workers in about two weeks. The archive total moved from 2,189 notices and 254,709 workers to 2,195 and 255,368. Official filings in the last 365 days rose from 71 to 74, and their worker total from 8,388 to 8,917. Future effective dates increased from 13 to 16, and the newest official notice date moved from October 7 to October 30.

Virginia's position in the national ranking has also firmed up. On November 3, the state entered the top five with 2,373 workers in the 90-day window. Five days later that figure is 2,546, a gain of 173 workers, and fifth place has firmed up. Virginia's 90-day total is small next to California's 21,514 and Washington's 17,411, but it leads every state outside the top four.

Virginia's recent history shows how uneven layoff years can be. The pandemic year of 2020 dominates the archive: 453 notices and 58,089 workers, nearly five times the worker total of any year since. The years after that fell back sharply — 74 notices and 7,852 workers in 2021, then 60 and 5,220 in 2022. 2023 brought 112 notices and 9,742 workers, 2024 brought 63 and 8,706, and 2025 brought 112 and 12,067.

Further back, totals ran higher: 132 notices and 17,968 workers in 2019, 188 and 19,216 in 2018, 121 and 12,261 in 2017, and 118 and 15,058 in 2016. By that longer view, 2026 is a quiet year for Virginia — below every year from 2012 through 2019, and below 2023 and 2025 as well.

Two Virginia filings stand out in the recent record. The largest is The Washington Post's 118-worker filing for its Springfield, Virginia operation, with notice given on October 7, 2026 and an effective date of December 31, 2026. It is the single biggest Virginia filing in the current window.

The newest is Haynes Furniture's 83-worker filing, with notice on October 30, 2026 and an effective date of November 2, 2026 — a notice gap of just three days, far shorter than the 60 days the WARN Act generally requires. That short gap is the kind of detail worth watching: the law allows shorter notice only in narrow circumstances, such as unforeseeable business circumstances or a faltering company seeking capital.

Both filings sit inside a national calendar whose heaviest week ahead is November 15, with 7,887 workers scheduled, followed by November 22 with 6,614. The newest week, December 13, carries 5,307. Virginia's 16 future effective dates are part of that pipeline, including the Washington Post filing effective on the last day of the year.

Three caveats matter. First, these are reported plans, not confirmed job losses. A WARN notice records what an employer told the state it intends to do; some planned layoffs shrink, some are averted, and some workers are rehired. Second, Virginia's archive blends 1,138 direct government records with 1,057 secondary records, so coverage depends on what sources report and what the registry has imported. A quiet feed is not proof of zero layoffs. Third, the 90-day window shows the current pace, while the full archive shows the state's longer history.

What the numbers do show is a state near the top of the national ranking on a moderate 2026 pace by its own history, with small recent filings and a modest pipeline of 16 future effective dates. The story to watch is whether the heavy November and December weeks bring new Virginia filings with them.

'Dubious Ownership': The 2022 Git CVE Behind the Error Your Agent's Container Hits

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 8 • Edited on Nov 8 • AI-assisted

Claude Code rejects --dangerously-skip-permissions when it's launched as root, per its dev container docs, so the container you build for an agent that runs without permission prompts uses a non-root user, say node at uid 1000. Your host account is uid 1001, and you start the container with plain docker run, the repo bind-mounted from the host. The agent's first git status comes back with this:

That is the message template in Git's setup.c, with /work as the path. The fix it suggests is one line. The tempting move is to go one step further, paste safe.directory '*' into a Dockerfile or your own ~/.gitconfig, and never see the error again.

That also switches off the protection. The check is there because of a real 2022 vulnerability, and the situation it guards against is the one you build when you give an agent its own user or container.

On a Linux host, file ownership is a number. A bind mount shows the container the same number the host has. Your repo is owned by your uid, 1001 in the example above, and git inside the container checks it against its own. The quickest way to see a mismatch needs no custom user at all: Docker runs containers as root (uid 0) by default, and 0 isn't 1001, so git stops.

The alpine/git image runs git with whatever arguments you pass:

I haven't run this exact command myself. What git checks is in its source: is_path_owned_by_current_uid calls lstat on the path and compares the file's owner with geteuid(). When git runs as root it also accepts the uid in SUDO_UID, which sudo sets and docker run does not. Docker Desktop handles bind mounts its own way, so on macOS or Windows you may not see the error at all.

The non-root user from the opening fails the same way: node at 1000 inside the container, files owned by 1001 on the host, same message.

The check comes from CVE-2022-24765. When you run git in a folder, it walks up the parent directories looking for a .git. On a shared machine, someone else could create one above you. The Git 2.30.3 release notes give the examples: a repository in C:\.git, on a mounted network drive, or in a scratch space.

That matters because some config values run programs. GitHub's announcement names core.fsmonitor, which makes git execute a command. Plant a C:\.git\config with that value, and every user who runs git in a folder under C:\ that isn't its own repo runs your command. They don't even have to type git. The release notes point out that a Git-aware shell prompt running git status, or opening the folder in VS Code or Atom, was enough.

CVE-2022-24765: a .git planted in a parent folder could run its owner's commands when you ran git below it.

Vendor docs followed. Atlassian's article lists both wordings. GitHub's actions/checkout gained a set-safe-directory input, on by default, which runs git config --global --add safe.directory for the one workspace path.

The git-config docs put it plainly: "To completely opt-out of this security check, set safe.directory to the string *." Every repository on the machine is then treated as safe, whoever owns it.

Two details in the same docs matter for agents. safe.directory is only read from protected configuration, meaning system, global and command scope, so a repo can't vouch for itself. And a value ending in /* allows every repository under that directory, which is a middle ground but still broader than one path.

In a throwaway container that only ever mounts one repo, * costs you little. There is no second user inside to plant anything. The damage comes when it leaks out of there: into your host ~/.gitconfig because the error showed up once, into a base image the whole team builds on, or onto a shared CI runner.

A second OS user for the agent, a shared box you reach over SSH, a container user writing into a folder your host user also works in: each is the multi-user machine the CVE was written for. The less-trusted user is the agent, and Anthropic's own docs warn that, run with --dangerously-skip-permissions, a dev container doesn't stop a malicious project from exfiltrating anything accessible inside it. If the agent's user can create a .git with core.fsmonitor in a directory above where you later run git, the ownership check is what keeps that command from running as you. With * in your global config, nothing does.

Match the uid. If git runs as the user who owns the files, there's nothing to fix. With plain Docker, pass your own ids. --user takes uid:gid, per the Docker run reference:

Dev containers do this for you on Linux. The spec's updateRemoteUserUID rewrites the container user's UID/GID "to match the local user's UID/GID to avoid permission problems with bind mounts," when a remoteUser or containerUser is set, and it defaults to true.

Matching the uid keeps the check on, but git can no longer tell the agent's files from yours, so on that mount the container is the boundary. The check never covered your repo's own .git anyway: you own it, and an agent with write access to the bind mount can add a core.fsmonitor value or a hook there. Look at any .git/config or .git/hooks change the agent made before you run git on the host.

List one path, not all of them. When the uids can't match, name the mount point. The command scope counts as protected, so -c works for a single run:

An agent runs git many times, so for a whole session pass it as environment variables. GIT_CONFIG_COUNT and the matching GIT_CONFIG_KEY_ and GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_ are command scope too, per the git-config docs:

Bake the path into the image. If every container mounts the repo at the same place, one line in the Dockerfile covers it, and it lives in the image's system config, so your host config never sees it:

If someone already put safe.directory=* in the system config, the git-config docs say to start your own list with an empty value. That resets it, and the paths you add after it are the only ones trusted.

Tools that run agents in parallel, Hermes included, often give each task its own git worktree. A linked worktree has a .git file, not a directory, and the git-worktree docs say it points back to the main repository's .git/worktrees/ folder. By default that pointer is an absolute host path. Mount only the worktree into a container and the path it names doesn't exist in there.

Two ways around it. Mount the main repository too, at the same path it has on the host. Or link worktrees with relative paths: Git 2.48 added this, set with worktree.useRelativePaths or --relative-paths. The docs warn it enables the extensions.relativeWorktrees extension, which older Git versions can't read. Remember the July 2022 fix as well: the ownership check now covers the worktree, the .git directory and the .git file, so all three have to belong to the container's user, or the worktree path goes in safe.directory.

I build Hermes IDE, so weigh this part with that in mind. Hermes starts each task on its own hermes/ branch in its own worktree, and every agent runs in a real terminal on your machine, as you. The agent's git and your git are the same user there, so the check never fires. The boundary comes in when you add one: an SSH session to a shared box (Hermes supports those), or a container you start from a plain shell session in the task's worktree. That's when the three fixes above apply.

The Review Desk's Worktrees tab: one Hermes worktree each for orders-api, web-app and orders-types under ~/code, with each worktree's hermes/ branch, task, age and disk size, and the free disk space.

Before you add anything, see where a * might already be hiding. git config --show-origin --get-all safe.directory prints every entry with the file it came from. Entries in a repo's local config show up in that list too, but git ignores them for this check, per the git-config docs. A * in your global or system file is the one to replace with a path.

Giving an agent its own user, container or worktree is a guardrail, and every guardrail has an edge like this one where it rubs against older security assumptions. The AI Agents Pocket Guide covers the patterns behind autonomous agents, including how much autonomy to grant and where to put the guardrails. It's one of 8 short books in the Pocket Guides for Developers series, on agents, RAG, prompts, observability, events, databases and system design.

If you run several agents on one repo, Hermes IDE is free, source-available (BSL 1.1), on macOS, Windows and Linux, and on GitHub. It's not affiliated with Nous Research, Anthropic, OpenAI or any agent vendor.

This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

Bitcoin developer Cory Fields has an unusual idea for dealing with future quantum thieves: Make them risk their own Bitcoin every time they try to steal someone else’s.

In a November 4 post to the Bitcoin development mailing list, Fields outlined “quantum landmines” and two types of auctions designed to turn attacks on vulnerable wallets into expensive gambles. He called the ideas “thought experiments,” not a completed Bitcoin upgrade.

Bitcoin owners use a secret private key to authorize transactions. A matching public key lets the network check their signatures without learning the secret.

If the whole idea seems too hard to understand, we used AI to generate a video to help you grasp it:

Today’s computers cannot realistically calculate that private key from the public one. A sufficiently powerful quantum computer could use a mathematical technique called Shor’s algorithm to do it, potentially letting a thief spend coins as if they owned them.

Some older Bitcoin outputs have exposed public keys, and certain newer formats reveal them too. Other addresses hide the key until someone spends, so the risk is not identical for every wallet.

Researchers call the hypothetical arrival of a computer capable of these attacks “Q-Day.” No publicly demonstrated machine can steal Bitcoin this way today, and nobody knows exactly when one might exist.

Imagine someone owns 1 BTC in an old, potentially vulnerable wallet. Before Q-Day, this person creates a tiny, separate quantum-safe Bitcoin output containing a hidden proof that they controlled the original private key while quantum theft was still impossible.

Think of it as a sealed ownership receipt. Outsiders can see the small output, but not the evidence tucked inside it, or even that it is a trap.

Now imagine a future thief uses a quantum computer to recover the user’s old private key. Under Fields’s suggested rules, the thief cannot simply take her 1 BTC: They must add 1 BTC of their own from a quantum-safe wallet as collateral.

Both coins enter a restricted output worth 2 BTC. The thief must wait through a challenge period—30 days in Fields’s example—before spending it freely.

The wallet owner notices the transaction and reveals the pre-Q-Day proof. The network then allows the owner to move the full 2 BTC to the quantum-safe address they committed to earlier: their original coin, plus the thief’s collateral.

The attacker has no way to know in advance which old wallets contain these hidden receipts. Even a successful quantum key recovery could become a 1 BTC loss.

The wallet owner would also need matching collateral to initiate a normal post-Q-Day move of her own legacy coin. If they have no spare BTC, the same rule intended to hurt thieves could make moving her savings difficult.

Landmines only work when an owner prepares one before Q-Day and reveals it in time. They cannot protect coins whose keys are already lost, including some of the early Bitcoin holdings widely believed to be inaccessible.

Fields proposes a second mechanism called a staked auction. Before Q-Day, someone could lock up quantum-safe Bitcoin and secretly register a bid against a specific old wallet they expect thieves to target.

Suppose an apparently abandoned wallet holds 50 BTC. A future attacker tries to move it and posts another 50 BTC as collateral; during the auction period, someone who committed a bid in advance can challenge the attempted theft.

In Fields’s example, a successful challenger claims part of the attacker’s collateral while the original vulnerable coins are destroyed rather than awarded to the bidder. The point is to deprive thieves of a reliable payday, not return the coins to a missing owner.

His third idea, a sats auction, requires no advance stake. Instead, a vulnerable-coin spend would trigger a contest in which the highest bidder gets the coins minus their bid, while the runner-up pays their bid and gets nothing.

That resembles a notorious auction game where two bidders keep raising their offers because neither wants to be the person who loses money for nothing. A thief might avoid starting a contest that could cost more than the coins they hoped to steal.

One proposed response to quantum risk is to restrict spending from outdated wallets after a migration deadline. That might prevent theft, but could also permanently lock out legitimate owners who never moved their coins.

Fields is exploring a different trade-off: Keep old coins potentially spendable, but make attempted transfers costly and open to challenges. Bitcoin cannot tell an honest owner from a quantum thief if both can produce the same valid signature.

The trade-off is severe, considering that honest owners might need large collateral deposits, inactive holders could miss their chance to object, and auction rules might burn coins belonging to people who are still alive and simply offline.

Bitcoin would first need a quantum-safe way to hold and spend funds. Wallets would then need to support hidden ownership commitments, while Bitcoin’s consensus rules would have to enforce collateral, waiting periods, special recovery transactions, and auctions.

Developers would also need to agree on when the new rules activate, how much collateral is required, and what happens to losing bids or leftover funds. Fields believes some mechanics could be introduced through a soft fork, a type of Bitcoin rule change, but has not supplied the detailed design.

None of these protections exists in Bitcoin today. Fields has published no implementation or activation schedule, and his original post leaves the auction payouts and even the desired outcome of an auction unresolved.

They Want to Gene-Edit the World’s Deadliest Animal and Release It Into the Wild. What Happens Next Could Save Millions of Lives.

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

More pests might actually be the answer, researchers believe.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

It sounds like something out of science fiction: laboratories filled with thousands of genetically engineered animals kept in cages, all bred for the eventual purpose of being released into the wild. But that’s exactly what is happening at research institutions across Europe and North America where scientists are racing to create custom organisms—and save millions of lives.

Mosquitoes are the deadliest animal in the world. They kill around one million people every year by spreading malaria, dengue, yellow fever, and other deadly vector-borne diseases. Even as we try to protect ourselves with insect repellents, bed nets, and medications, they continue to be extremely effective at making us sick. Research suggests this problem will only get worse: rising temperatures and milder winters create the perfect conditions for the insects to increase their geographical range and active season. Meanwhile, the blood-suckers are developing resistance to many of our defenses.

In the face of this growing threat, experts are racing to find new ways to combat our small-but-mighty foe. At Target Malaria, an international research consortium headquartered in London working on genetic technologies to control malaria, scientists are using a groundbreaking gene-editing method to produce sterile female Anopheles gambiae mosquitoes, the primary vector for the transmission of human malaria. Diminishing the species’ population—especially in Africa, which accounts for 95 percent of malaria deaths, mostly in children under 5—could combat the deadly disease. However, some skeptics are concerned that this may open a door that can’t be shut.

To do this, researchers are using CRISPR-Cas9, which allows them to selectively modify DNA at precise locations in the genome, to induce something called a gene drive. This technology essentially bypasses the standard Mendelian system of inheritance—think back to your high school biology lecture on pea plants.

Like humans, mosquitoes have two copies of every gene. Some traits are dominant, which means they’re expressed even if an individual only has one copy of the gene. Others are recessive, which means both copies of the gene must be present for the gene to express itself. The gene drive skews the usual rules of inheritance so that a targeted trait—in this case, the gene related to infertility—is inherited at a higher rate. “If you disrupt only one copy of the gene, the mosquitoes are still fertile. But when you target both copies, the mosquitoes become sterile,” explains Alekos Simoni, PhD, a molecular biologist and technical support specialist at Target Malaria.

The thought is that, once researchers have enough mosquitoes with single copies of the gene drive, they’ll release the insects into the wild. Then, these mosquitoes will mate and pass on the mutation. This process will repeat with each generation, eventually causing a snowball effect.

“It’s a two-step process,” Simoni says. “So you first spread the gene drive, and when there are enough mosquitoes with the gene drive mating with each other, you produce sterile mosquitoes.” Eventually, “the population progressively declines,” he continues.

It seems like a straightforward plan in theory—but genetically engineering mosquitoes is a painstaking process. First, the researchers use a tiny needle to introduce the mutation in a mosquito embryo under a microscope. The scientists also include a fluorescent marker within the introduced genetic material that makes the modified mosquitoes glow in the dark, allowing them to easily track the insects. Then, they breed the modified mosquitoes and keep them in carefully controlled temperature, humidity, and light conditions.

“Then, we run a lot of different studies,” Simoni says. Researchers analyze how the gene drive transmits to the progeny, whether it impacts their behavior, and what the implications of releasing them into the wild might be.

Using sterilization to control insect populations is nothing new. A method called the Sterile Insect Technique (SIT) has been used since the 1950s to control populations of pests such as fruit flies and screwworm flies. In recent decades, it has been used in the United States to control Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, which can spread dengue, Zika, and chikungunya. SIT doesn’t involve any genetic engineering—it uses radiation to sterilize male mosquitoes that are then released into the wild. When these mosquitoes mate with a female, she will not produce any viable offspring.

Scaling SIT, however, is difficult. “You need to release 10 to 20 times as many males as the target population you want to control,” says Simoni, “and it also works very poorly in rural areas where there is very little infrastructure.” A gene drive, on the other hand, is based on releasing a relatively small number of mosquitoes, because it’s a process that spreads from generation to generation.

The latter method is still in its early stages—no gene-drive mosquitoes have been released anywhere yet. The first field trials will probably take place on islands or small areas, Simoni says, and will involve the release of mosquitoes on the order of thousands. Target Malaria previously had an approved release scheduled in the West African country of Burkina Faso, but the national authorities halted the effort.

Still, the work shows promise. In a 2024 study published in Nature Communications, researchers used a model to simulate the release of gene-drive mosquitoes in 16 areas across West Africa, each roughly 4,630 square miles in size. (For reference, Connecticut has a land area of roughly 4,842 square miles.) The team found that these releases could reduce the abundance of disease-carrying mosquitoes by 72 to 98 percent. Meanwhile, other unrelated teams are also using gene drives to engineer mosquitoes that are resistant to malaria parasites, which are what infect the insect in the first place.

Some skeptics have raised concerns over the potential use of gene-drive mosquitoes. After all, once you release a gene drive into the wild, you can’t easily reverse it. We don’t know how local ecosystems and food webs will respond to a decline in mosquito populations. Accidentally spreading organisms that carry the gene drive is also a major risk, according to a proposed code of ethics published in The CRISPR Journal. The technology also raises a more philosophical question: Is it morally acceptable for humans to potentially eradicate a species because it causes us harm?

Simoni thinks about this differently. He believes that it’s also an ethical concern to not intervene when there are tools available to solve a threat as deadly as malaria. “It’s always a matter of risks and benefits,” he says. When we’re thinking about a health problem that kills hundreds of thousands of people each year, “the ethical questions are: How can we stop it? If we have something that could potentially make an impact, why not use it?”

At the same time, researchers working on gene drives know that their work must be conducted responsibly. For instance, the team that wrote the aforementioned code of ethics recommends proactively incorporating ecological risk assessment into gene drive research. They also emphasize transparency, like conducting replicable studies and partnering with outside experts to mitigate any adverse effects of the work.

Simoni also stresses that gene-drive mosquitoes won’t be a silver bullet against vector-borne diseases. “A gene drive is something very promising,” Simoni says. “It’s a novel technology that has a lot of potential to make an impact, but not alone. It has to be used in combination with new vaccines, bed nets, or insecticides. But it can really make a difference.”

Ocean Spray Just Gave Canned Cranberry Sauce A Chocolate Makeover

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Someone finally saw the potential in those jiggly, ridged slices.

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

Canned cranberry sauce has some pretty stiff competition at Thanksgiving. Between the mashed potatoes, stuffing, and mac and cheese, that jiggly little side dish often gets treated like the ugly stepsister of the dinner table. And it probably doesn't help that it comes out looking exactly like the can it was packaged in.

But perhaps a little chocolate could improve its reputation.

Ocean Spray and Los Angeles chocolatier Compartés have teamed up with recipe developer Carolina Gelen on a new treat called Chocolate Covered Cranberry Sauce, taking the familiar Ocean Spray stuff and turning it into chocolate-coated discs, sold in packs of four.

If that sounds vaguely familiar, you may have seen Gelen's original Instagram video from 2023, when she experimented with dipping slices of canned cranberry sauce in chocolate (the recipe has since racked up more than 10 million views).

Ocean Spray's Kelvin Vuong said Gelen's recipe was exactly the kind of unexpected use for cranberry sauce the company wanted to share with more people. The brand eventually partnered with her and Compartés to turn the homemade recipe into something people could purchase—and Gelen is pretty thrilled to see her original experiment come this far.

"Getting to work with Ocean Spray and Compartés to turn that idea into something people across the country can try is incredibly exciting," she said in the announcement.

As for why this combination works, Compartés' creative director and chief chocolatier, Jonathan Grahm, pointed to the contrast between the cranberries' sharp, sour flavor and the richness of the chocolate. It's the same reason tart fruit and chocolate can work so well together, even if cranberry sauce isn't exactly the first thing you'd think to dip.

The limited-edition chocolates are available exclusively at Compartés for $24.95 per four-pack, while supplies last, with the gourmet chocolate brand also making chocolate turkeys filled with Ocean Spray's whole-berry cranberry sauce in both milk and dark chocolate options.

Cranberry sauce might never get the same love as mashed potatoes, but chocolate certainly gives it a fighting chance.

After your chair, a sitting to standing desk that's easy to raise and lower is the best investment you can make in your home office setup. The 50+ Best Gifts for Wives, According to a Wife Who Gets Paid to Shop

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you purchase an independently reviewed product or service through a link on our website, Rolling Stone may receive an affiliate commission.

Your wife is your greatest gift, and yes, even better than that Nintendo Game Boy you got in the early 2000s. Now it’s time to show her just how much you value her with a top-notch present this holiday season. I know they say money can’t buy love, but sometimes, I prefer mine in the shape of a brand-new designer bag. Sue me.

Shopping for the woman who has it all (and yes, that includes you) isn’t easy. Do you know her clothing size? Whether she’s a gold or silver jewelry girl? Is she stacking bracelets every morning, or has she worn the same diamond studs since 2017?

I shop for a living, and I’m also a wife with a husband of my very own, so I’ve got the professional credentials and field experience to help you out. Everything on this list is something I’ve personally used, owned, been gifted, or bought for myself. If it made the cut, it has my ultimate stamp of approval. And if I know women, and I do, she’ll approve, too.

There’s something for every budget, hobby, and personality, along with a few unique finds that’ll have her wondering, Who is your source? Don’t worry. I’ll never tell.

Below, I’ve compiled the ultimate guide to the 50+ best gifts to give your wife this holiday season. A quick reminder: Christmas is on Dec. 25, and Hanukkah begins on Dec. 4, which means both are approaching faster than you think. Shipping cutoffs are typically about two weeks before the big day, so now is the time to start shopping. I’ll also be updating this list throughout the next few months, so check back for even more finds worth buying.

Pick one of these unique gifts for your wife and watch her eyes light up like Santa himself just brought her the top pick on her wish list. And the best part? You get to take all the credit.

Just like the Grinch, my heart also grows three sizes when I know I’ve helped you find something your wife will absolutely love. Every year, I crown one product my personal holy grail: The thing I can’t stop thinking about, recommending, or, in this case, wearing since the moment I opened it. For 2026, that honor goes to this single diamond pendant from Brilliant Earth. At just under one carat, it’s classic, understated, and serves as compelling evidence that diamonds really are a girl’s best friend.

We both know your wife has and always will be THAT girl, so get her one of these ultra-viral Parke sweatshirts that all the other trendsetters are sporting. She’ll be impressed that you know what’s happening on the World Wide Web outside of your fantasy lineup.

If I were there in person, I’d be holding your hand while telling you this: Yes, she does want a Lola Blanket. If I had to guess, those wildly viral cozy throws have been sitting in her favorites for months. She just doesn’t want to pull the trigger because of the price. But take it from someone who owns four: They’re worth every single cent. Consider it an investment in your marriage (and your side of the bed).

I don’t know how else to explain this to you other than: She wants this. I know she does. Ralph Lauren’s bear sweaters are just as iconic as your wife’s personality, and while she might be a little puzzled about how you knew to get her something so specific, don’t worry. I’ll let you keep your secret.

The Fenty Beauty x CASETiFY collab was one of the coolest drops I tried in 2026. It brings together two brands that really have their fingers on the pulse of what women actually want. This clever little case attaches to the back of her phone, giving her a place to stash her favorite gloss and powder.

The original Lenox Spice Village sets first came out in the Eighties, and who knows, maybe her grandma even owned one. But recently, social media went into a frenzy over their re-release, and if I had to guess, your wife was right there with everyone else. Get her the complete set for a little under $300.

My UGG slippers are one of my most-worn possessions because they’re just as good for lounging around the house as they are for little errands, like walking the dog. They’re cozy enough to wear all day but practical enough to take outside. And yes, there is a difference between indoor and outdoor shoes. Don’t question it.

Cozy Earth pajamas have been a top seller in all of our holiday gift guides, and it’s not hard to see why. I own more than eight pairs of these cozy sets, and if it were up to me, I’d never take them off. Going to the office included. But an HR clothing violation seems like more work than it’s worth.

Some tropes are really just true. Like the fact that she wants jewelry for Christmas. GLDN makes gorgeous custom necklaces that let you personalize everything from her initial (or yours, if you’re feeling sentimental) to special charms and emblems. Plus, I wear mine every day, and it hasn’t tarnished or lost its shine. Consider this one a pretty safe bet.

Don’t worry, I fully believe you when you say you always make sure your wife finishes (🙄). But just in case she’s in the mood for an extra-unholy night, Womanizer’s Next Duo vibrator is worth adding to the mix. It specializes in G-spot activation, making it the gift that keeps on giving — for both of you.

You should hug your wife a hundred times a day. I don’t make the rules. But if you have things like a job, hobbies, and other obligations that prevent you from providing constant physical affection, this Bearaby weighted blanket is the next best thing. Fun fact: Whenever someone sleeps over at my house, this is the most-requested item for guests to borrow.

Ring, ring, ring. And no, I’m not calling you. I’m trying to get it through your head that this is exactly the kind of present that should be sitting under her tree. If you don’t know her ring size, don’t panic. The most common sizes are 5, 6, and 7, but even if you guess wrong (it’s okay, you tried!), Brilliant Earth offers free resizing.

In a perfect world, I’d love it if you showed up every single month with the most beautiful bundle of flowers to make her smile. Sure, that’s romantic, but it’s not always realistic. Instead, let Bouqs take care of it for you. The flower delivery service sends stunning stems on whatever schedule you choose. Look at you, being thoughtful all year long.

For my most recent wedding anniversary, my husband got me antique Spode china, and it’s honestly one of the best gifts I’ve ever received. Macy’s has this jaw-droppingly beautiful green set on sale, and it’s exactly the kind of present she’ll cherish for a lifetime. I know I will.

According to legends, pearls symbolize “enduring love,” which is definitely something you two share. Otherwise, you wouldn’t have clicked on this article. This sweet set from Kara Yoo features two authentic pearls and comes in either a gold or silver setting, depending on her jewelry preference.

Perfume can be a tricky gift because everyone has their own very specific scent preferences. But this J’adore Dior perfume (which I’ve been wearing for the last 10 years) is about as reliable as they come. It has a light, pretty profile, and I’ve convinced more people to buy it after smelling it on me than I can count. At this point, I’m practically Dior’s unofficial brand ambassador.

I’m truly obsessed with these Brilliant Earth gemstone rings. They’re fun thanks to their striking color options, but still incredibly refined, with that secret diamond halo we love peeking out from the sides. Pro tip: When choosing the right shade, think about more than just her favorite color (which you should know by now, by the way). Pay attention to the colors she actually wears, too. Feel free to do a little investigating in her closet while she’s away. I believe in you.

If your holiday plans are a little less nice and a little more naughty, get her this delicate lace set from For Love & Lemons. It’s one of my favorite brands, and the quality is unmatched. Lingerie is the kind of gift that can be wrapped up in a pretty little bow, even though the real present is what comes after. I’ll let you figure out the rest.

This Gorjana bracelet works for two types of wives: The ones who wear bracelets and the ones who don’t. Let me explain. For the woman who typically skips bracelets, it’s dainty and subtle enough that it won’t clash with her everyday jewelry. And for the woman who already wears them, it’ll blend seamlessly with everything else in her collection. You can personalize it with her birthstone, your child’s, yours, or simply whichever color you think she’d appreciate most. It’s thoughtful without requiring you to become a jewelry expert overnight.

A Peixoto bathing suit (which I own, by the way, and can confirm is just as flattering as it is comfortable), a round-trip ticket to paradise, and you by her side? That’s what I call a Christmas miracle. Just don’t forget to book the flights. The swimsuit is only half the surprise.

Mariah Carey enters the holiday season each year by declaring, “It’s tiiiiiime!” And we share the same sentiment, which is why you should get her this watch from Nixon. It’s a little less expensive than some of its competitor brands without sacrificing much in the way of quality.

Chanel makeup is my ultimate gift-giving hack. It feels like a really expensive present but is a little easier to swallow when it’s under $200. It also comes in gorgeous gift packaging for free, so make sure you click that option at checkout. And the shades I’ve picked here are flattering on just about every skin tone.

I’ve owned my Gardyn Studio for a few years now, and some plant aficionados might come after me for this, but I’d choose it over a traditional backyard garden every time. From our first harvest, I was able to grow six months’ worth of vegetables and make some of the best salads I’ve ever tasted. It’s a cool, interactive gift that doesn’t require her to have a green thumb. Even better, you’ll get to reap the rewards, too.

Your first always holds a special place in your heart… and, of course, I’m talking about American Girl dolls. She may not play with Kit and Felicity as she did in the early days, but Stoney Clover Lane just dropped an adorable new collection that pays homage to her childhood favorites without feeling too kitschy. Want to really seal the deal? Ask her mom which doll she had growing up. You’ll be husband of the damn year.

All wives deserve designer items. But instead of spending a few grand on a handbag, I’d suggest opting for sunglasses. The packaging alone makes them feel like an extremely luxurious gift, and I chose this particular pair for two reasons: One, Prada speaks for itself. And two, this specific frame looks good on practically every face shape. Not that you’d have to worry about that, because your wife is perfect. Obviously.

If she loves having someone else in bed with her just as much as she loves having you there (I’m talking about your pet, of course), then she needs a custom portrait of her four-legged companion. My husband surprised me with one last year, and it now hangs prominently in our room. He used this exact Etsy seller, and the portrait pictured here is of my dog, Alfie. Or should I say King Alfred of the Upper East Side?

Some wives are a little witchier than the rest (that’s WITCH with a “W”), so if she’s into all things tarot and spiritual, get her this necklace that holds miniature versions of tarot decks. Let me put it this way: If this is one of her hobbies, there’s no way she didn’t consult her own tarot deck before marrying you. And thankfully, it worked out. So you should probably support it.

If you’re a snorer, don’t worry. A lot of people are! One of the most selfless gifts you can give her is the ability to sleep through the night without hearing a thing, which is where Loop earplugs come in. I’m not saying I put mine in every night and no longer have to lie awake listening to my husband sound like a bear in hibernation, but I’m also not not saying that. Either way, she’ll thank you in the morning.

Remember all those times she came home from happy hour with the girls feeling a little… silly? Well, chances are, she was drinking one of these. The St-Germain Hugo Spritz is practically the unofficial drink of every girl right now, and it’s also one of my personal favorites. You may not get an invite to gossip with the gals, but you can at least make the drinks at home for her.

This hedgehog gardening can will make watering her flowers a little more whimsical, especially if your wife doesn’t take anything too seriously (except your marriage, of course).

Frances Valentine is known for its quirky creations that deliver designer-level quality, without the six-figure price tag. Open my closet, and you’ll find no shortage of these bags sitting there. One thing I especially love? They have a secure snap closure, so you don’t have to worry about your stuff falling out, along with gorgeous hardware. You might not know what that means, but she does. Trust me, she cares about these things.

These boots were definitely made for walking. I’m a Dolce Vita enthusiast, and not a single time have any of their footwear delights left me with a blister or fatigued feet. This is the brand she needs to know about, with boots that’ll quickly become her go-to for years to come. Because looking cute is one thing, but actually being able to walk in your shoes? That’s the real luxury.

Being a mob wife is really a state of mind, and if she loves all things glamorous, get her this faux fur coat from Abercrombie & Fitch. It’s plush and comes in multiple size options. The best part: You get to keep your own coat instead of handing it over to her every time you go outside. This one should keep her plenty warm.

Camp Snap is a fun little gadget that lets her take retro-feeling photos with the click of a button. It’s a great gift to pair with a vacation to somewhere like, oh, I don’t know, Paris, so she can really put it to good use. Consider this your not-so-subtle hint to book the trip, too.

In my personal and professional opinion, one of the most romantic things you can do for your spouse is recreate a little of that same childhood Christmas magic they experienced growing up. An advent calendar is a great way to do just that. This one from Glasshouse Fragrances is filled with goodies that are a little more luxe than a themed lollipop.

If she has a record player, vinyl is a great gift that you can keep giving. And if she doesn’t, well, now you have another gift idea. Maybe she was a Twilight girl in her youth, in which case you can grab her the official soundtrack. But if vampires weren’t her thing, no judgment. Urban Outfitters has a massive vinyl selection for you to browse.

For the wife who needs a second to herself and wants to drown out the noise around her (of course, she’d never want to do that to you), get her a pair of Bose QuietComfort headphones. They come in multiple colors, and here’s another selling point: I occasionally wear glasses, so if your wife is also a glasses wearer, she can rest assured these won’t irritate her ears the way some other brands can.

Yes, coffee in bed is great, but coffee that stays warm throughout the day is arguably even better. Ember’s smart mugs take care of that for her, so if she’s anything like me and takes forever to finish one cup, it’ll still be at the perfect temperature when she finally gets around to that last sip.

Glassware, to me, is the grown-up version of asking for Barbies for Christmas. You can never seem to get enough of it, and every set has its own personality. This glittering option from Anthropologie lets you build your own bundle, so if she’s more of a Champagne drinker, add a few extra flutes. If margaritas are her thing, throw in some coupes. The choice is yours.I also especially love Anthropologie glassware because I’ve been known to break a dish or two (or three) in my time, and its pieces are surprisingly sturdy and resistant to chipping.

She already keeps your pet close to her heart, but now she can keep them even closer with this custom sweatshirt from Uncommon Goods. You can add multiple pets, their names, and even choose the color of the crewneck. When my husband gifted this to me, I pretty much cried. Which also annoyed my dog, who promptly left the room.

If hitting the slopes is one of your favorite hobbies to do together, show her how much those Saturday and Sunday ski trips really mean to you by getting her this cool candle from Uncommon Goods. For added authenticity, it’s handmade in Colorado, aka home to some of the best powder in the country.

Would a daily foot massage be her preference? Probably. But another good alternative is these recovery slides from Nike. They’re designed to help her feet feel better, and you don’t have to touch her toes. Everybody wins.

As I’ve already mentioned (and in case it wasn’t clear enough), jewelry is a great gift. But if you want to get her something that can also help support her health and wellness, an Oura Ring is the way to go. I primarily use mine to help keep track of my sleep, especially when my insomnia acts up (because sadly, counting sheep just doesn’t cut it).

So, I don’t want to lie to you: I’m not the cook in the house. It’s for the good of the group. But I did gift these pans to my husband, who has a real passion for the culinary arts, and he said, and I quote, “It changed the way I work in the kitchen.” Selfishly, I’ve reaped all the benefits. So technically, this is a gift for both of you.

Myles Price makes some of the coziest sets in the business, but they’re a little more polished than your average loungewear find. One product tip: I’d recommend going up a size. I typically wear a small sweatshirt, but I much prefer the medium for a little extra room.

One of the most considerate gifts you can give is something that looks out for her health and well-being, which is why I’m a big fan of adding Timeline supplements to her stocking. They’re filled with all sorts of good-for-you ingredients designed to help her body feel its best.And if she loves them (which I know she will), the brand also offers subscription options.

I love a Disney adult. In fact, I worked at the happiest place on earth for a year, so I feel pretty qualified to say that. If your wife shares the same sentiment but doesn’t necessarily want to wear a pair of Mickey ears during her daily doings, this Barefoot Dreams cardigan is a much chicer way to show it. Like all of the brand’s products, it’s ridiculously soft and has just a touch of Minnie magic on the back.

You know what they say: The bigger the bag, the bigger the thank-you. Okay, no one actually says that, but they should. So when you gift your wife this Dagne Dover tote, I have a feeling she’ll be eternally grateful when she realizes just how much of her stuff she can actually fit inside without the usual struggle.

If you really want to show your wife you know about her passions (and in this case, it’s cooking), get her a special olive oil from famed restaurant Estiatorio Milos. It makes a fabulous stocking stuffer when paired with some fresh pasta and a bottle of wine. Because the greatest gift is making someone feel seen, and this shows her you’ve actually been paying attention to what she loves. Look at you, partner of the year.

Anyone who knows me knows I live in my Aviator Nation sweatsuits. They wash better than just about any other piece of clothing I’ve ever owned, never lose their comfort, and, thanks to that iconic three-stripe design, are recognizable from a mile away. Your wife will know exactly what she’s unwrapping, and she’ll probably be wearing it by the end of the day.You can even treat yourself to a matching set while you’re at it. Being the ultimate partner is exhausting work, and you deserve a little spoiling, too.

Elix makes wellness products designed to support everything from better sleep to stress relief, which can come in particularly handy after all the holiday chaos. Stuff a few into her stocking or go all in with a larger bundle. Either way, she’ll wonder how she ever lived without them. Another bonus: I’ve been using these products for a while now, and whenever I’ve come down with a cold or been hit with a bout of fatigue, they’ve helped me get back on my feet. Consider it a little extra TLC when she needs it most (so maybe after dinner with your parents).

Nov. 8, 1656: The birth of Edmond Halley

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s been said that the general public knows three astronomical objects: the Moon, the rings of Saturn, and Halley’s Comet. English astronomer Edmond Halley, born Nov. 8, 1656, didn’t discover the object named for him. In fact, he never even saw it — but his mathematical prediction of its return in 1758 guaranteed his place in history.

Halley was a friend of Isaac Newton and used Newton’s theory of gravity and to calculate “his” comet’s orbit. In fact, Halley published at his own expense the book Newton wrote explaining the theory, Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica. In 1676, Halley sailed to the island of St. Helena in the South Atlantic Ocean and spent a year measuring stellar positions, the result of which was the first catalog of the southern sky.

Michael E. Bakich is a contributing editor for Astronomy magazine. With degrees in astronomy and planetarium education, he has led many eclipse tours, published several books on observing, and logged thousands of hours at the eyepiece.

Is a Market Crash Coming? Warren Buffett's Warning Is Ringing Loud and Clear.

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buffett has successfully invested through all kinds of markets over decades and is worth listening to.

Is a market crash coming? That's the question on many investors' minds today as the strong bull market continues. The S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%) has more than doubled over the past four years, and it's up 12% year to date. Artificial intelligence (AI) euphoria is still driving the market, but there are signs that suggest a crash could be on the way.

Consider what Warren Buffett recently said about the state of the market today.

Warren Buffett is arguably the greatest investor of his generation. He recently stepped down as CEO of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -0.01%)(BRKB -0.25%) after leading it for 60 years, during which time its stock gained 5,502,284%, vs. 39,054% for the S&P 500.

Warren Buffett. Image source: Getty Images.

Buffett is a value investor, which means that he invests in undervalued stocks, or stocks trading beneath their intrinsic values. However, the most important thing he looks for in a stock is a great business. As he puts it, "It's far better to buy a wonderful company at a fair price than a fair company at a wonderful price." Note the word fair, which suggests that the stock isn't overpriced.

He has invested in all kinds of circumstances, including long bull markets, major crashes, high inflation, and just about everything else. Admirers look to him for guidance, and he gives advice freely.

What he said this past July should make every investor pay attention. In an interview with CNBC, he said, "It's tough to find values when everybody is preferring gambling." That was a follow-up to a remark he made in an interview in May, when he said, "I've compared the markets to a church with a casino attached."

Warren Buffett would be the first person to admit he doesn't know what's coming next. But decades of experience give him an edge in understanding how the market works.

Since this bull market started four years ago, Buffett built up Berkshire Hathaway's cash pile to its highest ever, nearly $400 billion. He was a net seller of stocks for many quarters while the market was rising and investors were pushing up some AI stocks to outrageous levels.

The entire time, Berkshire Hathaway found something to buy every quarter. However, when the market becomes very expensive and it seems people are gambling, it's even more important to pay attention to valuation and look for market bargains.

A market crash might be around the corner, and investors should consider emulating Buffett by having cash ready and investing in fairly priced stocks.

Jennifer Saibil has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Berkshire Hathaway. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Can a hacker really do anything with your IP address?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An IP address won’t hand over your passwords, but it can give attackers somewhere to aim

In November 2021, Twitch streamer xQc lost his internet connection shortly after playing "Crab Game." The game's developer then warned streamers to avoid public lobbies after discovering that the game could expose players' IP addresses to distributed denial-of-service (DDoS) attacks. Although xQc couldn't confirm an attack caused that specific outage, the developer soon switched to newer Steam networking to hide IP addresses.

IP addresses basically help route data to and from websites and games. Now, most home routers let multiple devices share one public IPv4 address, while IPv6 can give each device its own public-facing address. But before you pull out the tinfoil hat, understand that neither type of IP address gives someone access to your passwords or personal data. However, both can give a hacker a target to aim at, and that's enough to raise an eyebrow.

An IP lookup is a quick way to reveal your internet service provider and approximate city or region. Back in 2016, a Kansas family sued MaxMind after its database mapped approximately 650 million unrelated IP addresses to their farm, prompting repeated law enforcement visits over incidents involving people elsewhere.

A typical DDoS attack floods a target with traffic from multiple sources. It's the same kind of attack Bluesky blamed for its server outages, and on a smaller scale, it could interrupt gaming sessions, video calls, or even an entire household internet connection. A firewall may offer little help once the internet connection is overwhelmed.

To access a device, an attacker needs a way in, such as an exposed security camera, remote-access software with a security flaw, or a home server with weak password protection. IPv4 port forwarding or permissive IPv6 firewall settings can expose those services.

Some IPv6 addresses are used only within local or private networks. Temporary IPv6 addresses can change over time, making individual devices harder to track, although devices on the same home network may still share a recognizable address prefix.

IPv6 can give devices their own public IP addresses, which sounds riskier than it is. A properly configured router firewall can still block unsolicited connections while allowing responses to traffic initiated from inside your network.

An OpenWrt user on Reddit turned on IPv6, tested their home server remotely, and discovered that their remote-access and file-sharing ports were reachable from outside the network. Their firewall had been set to allow forwarding, and changing that setting to reject closed the opening.

In October 2026, Microsoft disclosed CVE-2026-105016, a critical vulnerability in its QUIC implementation. It could allow specially crafted IPv6 traffic to execute code in affected .NET applications on Windows. Microsoft's advisory hasn't mentioned whether attackers had exploited the flaw.

So rather than disabling IPv6 altogether, check your router's firewall settings for both protocols, remove unnecessary remote-access rules, and install security updates. Microsoft mentions that turning off IPv6 could result in problems with some of the Windows components. And while a VPN can hide your usual public IP address from websites if it handles both IPv4 and IPv6 traffic correctly, it isn't going to patch vulnerable software. It's also worth checking that your VPN is working before you rely on it.
